Constants of motion and fundamental frequencies for elliptic orbits at fourth post-Newtonian order
David Trestini

Abstract
In the case of nonspinning compact binary systems on quasi-elliptic orbits, I obtain the conservative map between the constants of motion (energy and angular momentum) and the fundamental (radial and azimuthal) frequencies at the fourth post-Newtonian order, including both instantaneous and tail contributions. This map is expressed in terms of an enhancement function of the eccentricity, which is appropriately resummed to ensure accuracy for any eccentricity; in particular, I recover known results for circular orbits. In order to obtain this map, the local dynamics are expressed using an action-angle formulation. The tail term is treated as a perturbation, which is first localized in time, then Delaunay-averaged. Both operations require a contact transformation of the phase-space variables, which I explicitly control. Using the first law of binary black hole mechanics, I then obtain the orbit-averaged redshift invariant for eccentric orbits at fourth post-Newtonian order; when properly accounting for the tail contributions, it perfectly agrees with analytical self-force at postgeodesic order [Phys. Rev. D 106, 044004 (2022)] . Finally, I use these results to re-express the fluxes of energy and angular momentum obtained at third post-Newtonian order in [Phys. Rev. D 77, 064035 (2008)] and [Phys. Rev. D 80, 124018 (2009)] in terms of fundamental frequencies.



I
Introduction

Compact binaries typically arise from two formation channels: isolated formation or dynamical formation. In the isolated formation scenario, one of the stars in the stellar binary collapses into a black hole, which is then engulfed into a common envelope formed by the remaining star. Due to drag forces, the orbits then tighten significantly, until the remaining star also collapses into a black hole, forming a binary black hole. The binary then evolves in isolation, gradually inspiralling and shedding its eccentricity due to gravitational-wave emission, such that it is essentially quasi-circular by the time it enters the LIGO-Virgo-KAGRA (LVK) frequency band. This scenario has motivated gravitational wave modelers to focus on quasi-circular binaries, and this has been mostly sufficient for the purposes of the LVK observatories. However, a certain number binaries exhibit hints of eccentricity
94
;
82
;
90
;
72
;
73
, and were probably formed dynamically
109
;
92
;
42
;
96
;
93
. In this scenario, a widely separated, eccentric black hole binary gets ‘hardened’ by its interaction with a third body, leading to a more compact, highly eccentric binary. Such systems are expected to be common in future gravitational wave detectors such as Einstein Telescope, Cosmic Explorer or LISA
42
;
95
;
36
. Moreover, LISA will detect many extreme and intermediate mass ratio inspirals (EMRIs and IMRIs), which will generically exhibit high eccentricity
3
. IMRIs, and to a lesser extent EMRIs, can be modeled by hybridizing black hole perturbation theory (and gravitational self-force) information at leading (and subleading) orders in the mass ratio with post-Newtonian information at higher orders in the mass ratio
65
;
64
. Analyzing signals from highly-eccentric binaries using quasi-circular waveform models can lead to significant biases in the source parameters
54
, so there is now a strong motivation to improve post-Newtonian gravitational waveform models for eccentric orbits.

In this work, I will focus on the conservative problem, ignoring dissipative radiation-reaction effects, which can be added separately in a second step. In the center-of-mass frame, a compact binary can be described gauge-invariantly in two ways. The first way is through the Noetherian constants of motion arising from the symmetries of the background Minkowski space-time. For nonspinning particles, there is no spin precession, so one can restrict to the orbital plane and characterize the binary by its energy
E
, associated with time translations, and the norm of its angular momentum
J
, associated with rotations. This description is useful because these are the quantities that enter flux-balance laws, up to small dissipative corrections, called Schott terms, that can be added separately
105
. Such flux-balance laws are critical to control the evolution of the system under radiation-reaction at high post-Newtonian orders. The second way to describe the system is through the fundamental frequencies of motion, here the radial frequency
n
(often denoted
\Omega_{r}
in a self-force context) and the azimuthal frequency
\omega
(or
\Omega_{\phi}
). These are useful because they are associated with observable effects such as the pericenter advance. Thus, in order to control the frequency (and phase) evolution at high post-Newtonian order, which is the main observable in a gravitational-wave signal, one needs to understand the link between the constants of motion and the fundamental frequencies.

In this work, I will establish this mapping at 4PN order, extending previous results at 4PN order for circular orbits
46
;
47
;
48
;
14
;
13
;
15
. For elliptic orbits, this relation was also previously obtained at 2PN in
44
;
98
and at 3PN in
81
. More recently, Ref.
39
obtained the local contribution to this relation at 4PN, but did not fully account for the hereditary tail contribution. Here, I completely control the tail contribution and obtain the full 4PN result, valid for arbitrary eccentricity. Note that the expression of the Schott term for eccentric orbits, which will be needed for the 4PN phasing for elliptic orbits, is left to future work. Moreover, note that partial results have been obtained at higher PN orders
17
;
18
;
16
.
I also study some applications of these results. First, I deduce from this map the 4PN redshift invariant for eccentric orbits using the first law of binary mechanics
75
;
76
;
29
;
21
. This invariant is extremely useful to make contact with gravitational self-force, and I found perfect agreement at 4PN with the post-geodesic order results of Ref.
85
; notably, this agreement is found analytically and for arbitrary eccentricity. I was also able to re-express the 3PN fluxes of energy and angular momentum in terms of the fundamental frequencies; for this, I apply this map to the results of Refs.
8
;
7
;
9
, which obtain these fluxes in terms of energy and angular momentum.

The structure of the paper is as follows. At the end of this introduction, I introduce relevant notation, then summarize and point to the various relations obtained in this work. In Sec.
II
, I provide reminders about the 4PN equations of motion, and how it derives from a Hamiltonian. In particular, I discuss the fundamental aspects of the localization of the hereditary piece of the Hamiltonian, which will prove essential to my approach. In Sec.
III
, I derive the action-angle formulation of the
local
4PN dynamics.
Sec.
IV
is then devoted to the inclusion of the corrections due to the hereditary tail.
The hereditary contribution is expressed in terms of an enhancement function
\Lambda_{0}(e)
, for which I propose a very accurate resummation.
Using the full Hamiltonian in action-angle form, I then straightforwardly obtain in Sec.
V
the map between the fundamental frequencies, the action variables and the conserved energy and angular momentum.
In Sec.
VI
, I use this map and the first law of binary black hole mechanics
75
;
76
;
29
to obtain the expression of the 4PN (orbit-averaged) redshift invariant on eccentric orbits.
In Sec.
VII
, I study the circular limit of these results by requiring the radial action to vanish.
Finally, using the results of Refs.
8
;
7
;
9
, I obtain in Sec.
VIII
the gauge invariant expressions of the orbit-averaged 3PN fluxes in terms of fundamental frequencies. I end with a discussion in Sec.
IX
.

I.1
Notations

Spatial vectors are denoted in bold font, and their spatial components are denoted with Latin indices; e.g.
\bm{u}
and
u^{i}
. Conversely, spacetime indices are denoted with Greek letters,
u^{\mu}=(u^{0},u^{i})
. The Kronecker delta is
\delta_{ij}
and the Levi-Civita symbol is
\epsilon_{ijk}
. The
n
-th derivative of a function of time
f(t)
is denoted
f^{(n)}(t)=\mathrm{d}^{n}f/\mathrm{d}t^{n}
. Orbit-averages are denoted
\langle f\rangle
, whereas the oscillatory piece is
\widetilde{f}
such that
f=\langle f\rangle+\widetilde{f}
.

Two compact objects of masses
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
are considered. The total mass is
m=m_{1}+m_{2}
, the reduced mass is
\mu=m_{1}m_{2}/m
and the symmetric mass ratio is
\nu=m_{1}m_{2}/m^{2}=\mu/m\in\ ]0,1/4]
.
Moreover, I follow the conventions of Ref.
30
for the masses: I choose to label the masses such that
m_{1}\geq m_{2}
, and thus define the relative mass difference as
\delta=(m_{1}-m_{2})/m=\sqrt{1-4\nu}\geq 0
. The ordinary (small) mass ratio is then
\epsilon=m_{2}/m_{1}
.

Each object is associated (in ADM coordinates) with the position vectors
\bm{y}_{1}(t)
and
\bm{y}_{2}(t)
. Similarly, for
A\in\{1,2\}
, one defines the velocity
\bm{v}_{A}=\mathrm{d}\bm{y}_{A}/\mathrm{d}t
, the acceleration
\bm{a}_{A}=\mathrm{d}\bm{v}_{A}/\mathrm{d}t
and the jerk
\bm{b}_{A}=\mathrm{d}\bm{a}_{A}/\mathrm{d}t
; the
relative
separation is then
\bm{x}_{12}=\bm{y}_{1}-\bm{y}_{2}
, the relative velocity is
\bm{v}_{12}=\bm{v}_{1}-\bm{v}_{2}
and the relative acceleration is
\bm{a}_{12}=\bm{a}_{1}-\bm{a}_{2}
. The norm of the separation vector is denoted by
r_{12}=|\bm{x}_{12}|
, alongside the unit vector
\bm{n}_{12}=\bm{x}_{12}/r_{12}
.

When expressing quantities in the center-of-mass frame, the “
12
” label is typically dropped. One then also typically describes the motion using polar coordinates
(r,\theta)
. In the orbital plane (characterized by
\theta=0
), the separation vector is decomposed as
\bm{x}=(r\cos\phi,r\sin\phi,0)
. The conjugate momentum associated with
\bm{x}
is denoted
\bm{p}
, whereas the momenta associated with
(r,\phi)
are
(p_{r},p_{\phi})
.

The radial action is denoted
I_{r}
, the azimuthal action
I_{\phi}
, the angular momentum
J
and the energy
E
(without the rest mass
m
). Note that here, one indeed has
J=I_{\phi}
. I also introduce the Delaunay variable
I_{r\phi}=I_{r}+I_{\phi}
. All these conserved quantities have reduced counterparts. The reduced energy is given by
\varepsilon=-2E/(m\nu c^{2})
and the reduced angular momentum is
j=-2J^{2}E/(G^{2}m^{5}\nu^{3})
; they are such that
\varepsilon=\mathcal{O}(1/c^{2})
and
j=\mathcal{O}(1)
. The reduced action variables are given by
i_{r}=I_{r}/(Gm^{2}\nu)
,
i_{\phi}=I_{\phi}/(Gm^{2}\nu)
, and
i_{r\phi}=I_{r\phi}/(Gm^{2}\nu)
.

In the case of planar motion, one is interested in the two fundamental frequencies: the radial frequency
n
and the azimuthal frequency
\omega
. These are associated with the radial period
P=2\pi/n
and the periastron advance
K=\omega/n
. I also introduce the very useful parameters
x=(Gm\omega/c^{3})^{2/3}
and
\iota=3x/(K-1)
, first introduced by Blanchet in Ref.
7
. These parameters are chosen such that at leading post-Newtonian order,
x\sim\varepsilon
and
\iota\sim j
. In the context of comparisons with gravitational self-force, I also introduce
y=(Gm_{1}\omega/c^{3})^{2/3}
and
\lambda=3y/(K-1)
. In the Newtonian problem, I often use the semi-major axis
a
and the eccentricity
e
.

II
Hamiltonian formulation for the 4PN dynamics of compact binaries

II.1
The 4PN equations of motion and their derivation from an action principle

The most straightforward description of the motion of a compact binary is through the equations of motion. In a given coordinate system, at any time (described by coordinate time
t
), each particle (labeled
A
) is assigned a position vector
\bm{y}_{A}(t)
and an associated velocity vector
\bm{v}_{A}(t)
. Generically, post-Newtonian motion is then constrained by an equation of the form

\displaystyle\bm{a}_{A}\equiv\frac{\mathrm{d}\bm{v}_{A}}{\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{F}[\bm{y}_{1},\bm{y}_{2},\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2}]\,,

(1)

where
\bm{a}_{A}
denotes the acceleration of particle
A\in\{1,2\}
and
\mathfrak{F}
is some
functional
of the phase-space trajectories
t\mapsto\bm{y}_{1}(t)
,
t\mapsto\bm{y}_{2}(t)
,
t\mapsto\bm{v}_{1}(t)
and
t\mapsto\bm{v}_{2}(t)
. It is sufficient to determine
\bm{a}_{1}
, because
\bm{a}_{2}
is then determined by switching the labels
1\leftrightarrow 2
. Up to 3.5PN order,
\mathfrak{F}
is in fact an ordinary function of the positions and velocities, such that the equations of motion (
1
) reduce to an ordinary differential equation on the phase-space vector
(\bm{y_{1}},\bm{y_{2}},\bm{v_{1}},\bm{v_{2}})
. However, it was first shown in
22
that, at 4PN,
\mathfrak{F}
contains a piece which can be expressed as an integral over the past history of the binary (thus preserving causality), and (
1
) then becomes an
integro-differential
equation. This feature is referred to as
hereditary
or
non-local-(in-time)
, and the particular integral appearing is named a
tail
.
In harmonic coordinates, the equations of motion read

\displaystyle\bm{a}_{1}=\bm{a}_{1}^{\text{loc}}+\bm{a}_{1}^{\text{tail}}\,,

(2)

where
\bm{a}_{1}^{\text{loc}}
is some complicated instantaneous function of the phase-space variables, and where the tail contribution reads
15

\displaystyle a_{1,\text{tail}}^{i}

\displaystyle=-\frac{8G^{2}\mathrm{M}}{5c^{8}}\Bigg\{y_{1}^{j}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\tau\,\ln\left(\frac{c\tau}{2b_{0}}\right)\,\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(7)}(t-\tau)-y_{1}^{i}\bigg[\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}\,\ln\left(\frac{r_{12}}{b_{0}}\right)\bigg]^{(3)}+\frac{n_{12}^{i}}{4m_{1}r_{12}}\mathrm{I}_{jk}^{(3)}\mathrm{I}_{jk}^{(3)}\Bigg\}\,,

(3)

where
\mathrm{M}=m+\mathcal{O}(1/c^{2})
is the ADM mass and
b_{0}
is an arbitrary constant time-scale which cancels out in the full acceleration.
Here,
\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(n)}
is the
n
-th time-derivative of the source quadrupole moment, which is given at leading (Newtonian) order by

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}_{ij}=\sum_{A\in\{1,2\}}m_{A}y_{A}^{\langle i}y_{A}^{j\rangle}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{c^{2}}\right)\,.

(4)

When taking time derivatives of the quadrupole moment, accelerations appear, which can be straightforwardly order-reduced using the Newtonian equations of motion (one of course neglects 5PN corrections).
Both the instantaneous and tail pieces of the acceleration contain conservative contributions (leading to effects such as the periastron advance) and dissipative contributions (leading to the inspiral of the binary). In the instantaneous piece, the conservative contributions come with an integer PN order (Newtonian, 1PN, 2PN, 3PN, and 4PN) whereas the dissipative pieces come with a half-integer PN order (2.5PN and 3.5PN). In the tail piece, such a split does not occur, but these two contributions can be disentangled by dividing this integral in time-even and time-odd contributions, which read
15

\displaystyle a_{1,\text{tail, cons}}^{i}

\displaystyle=-\frac{4G^{2}\mathrm{M}}{5c^{8}}\Bigg\{y_{1}^{j}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\tau\,\ln\left(\frac{c\tau}{2b_{0}}\right)\,\left[\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(7)}(t-\tau)-\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(7)}(t+\tau)\right]-2y_{1}^{i}\bigg[\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}\,\ln\left(\frac{r_{12}}{b_{0}}\right)\bigg]^{(3)}+\frac{n_{12}^{i}}{2m_{1}r_{12}}\mathrm{I}_{jk}^{(3)}\mathrm{I}_{jk}^{(3)}\Bigg\}\,,

(5a)

\displaystyle a_{1,\text{tail, diss}}^{i}

\displaystyle=-\frac{4G^{2}\mathrm{M}}{5c^{8}}y_{1}^{j}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}\tau\,\ln\left(\frac{c\tau}{2b_{0}}\right)\left[\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(7)}(t-\tau)+\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(7)}(t+\tau)\right]\,.

(5b)

Since this work is only concerned with conservative effects, I will only consider the conservative acceleration

\displaystyle\bm{a}_{1}^{\text{cons}}

\displaystyle=\bm{a}_{1}^{\text{inst, cons}}+\bm{a}_{1}^{\text{tail, cons}}\,,

(6)

where
\bm{a}_{1}^{\text{inst, cons}}
is simply obtained by keeping only the integer PN orders (i.e., even powers of
1/c
) in
\bm{a}_{1}^{\text{inst}}
.

Although the 4PN tail contribution was initially obtained
via
the metric arising from an asymptotic matching between the near and far zone
20
;
22
, it was later found that the conservative acceleration, including the tail term, derives from an action principle. This action can either be constructed in the ADM formalism
46
(where the hereditary part is guessed from the equations of motion) or derived entirely from scratch using the Fokker action
12
;
14
;
15
;
13
;
80
or EFT methods
56
;
59
. The full action for the relative motion in the center-of-mass frame can be split as
S=S_{\text{loc}}+S_{\text{tail}}
. The
local piece initially depends not only on the positions and velocities, but also on the accelerations
\bm{a}_{A}
and even the jerks
\bm{b}_{A}=\mathrm{d}\bm{a}_{A}/\mathrm{d}t
. However, terms that depend on
\bm{b}_{A}
are eliminated as total derivatives in the Lagrangian
12
, and terms which are at least quadratic in the acceleration are eliminated using the “double-zero” method
50
;
11
, such that one is left with an action which is at most linear in the accelerations — this last residual dependence in the accelerations is eliminated by suitable coordinate shifts
12
. Thus, the local action finally reads

\displaystyle S_{\text{loc}}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,L_{\text{loc}}\Bigl(\bm{x}(t),\bm{v}(t)\Bigr)\,.

The tail piece then reads

\displaystyle S_{\text{tail}}

\displaystyle=\frac{G^{2}\mathrm{M}}{5c^{8}}\ \mathrm{Pf}_{\frac{2r_{12}(t)}{c}}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}t\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}}{|t-t^{\prime}|}\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}(t)\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}(t^{\prime})\,,

(7b)

where
\mathrm{Pf}_{\tau_{0}}
denotes the Hadamard
partie finie

regularization
1
1

1

The
partie finie

62
;
27
;
46
is defined in terms of a scale
\tau_{0}
, and it is introduced to make the action explicitly symmetric in
t\leftrightarrow t^{\prime}
. Here, it will suffice to know that, for any
f(t)
which tends sufficiently fast to
0
in the
t\rightarrow\pm\infty
limit, one has the identity
12

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pf}_{\tau_{0}}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}\frac{f(t^{\prime})}{|t-t^{\prime}|}=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}\tau\ln\left(\frac{\tau}{\tau_{0}}\right)\bigg[f^{(1)}(t-\tau)-f^{(1)}(t+\tau)\bigg]\,.

of the integral associated with the timescale
\tau_{0}
and where
\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}
here denotes the third time derivative of
\mathrm{I}_{ij}
.
For now, these derivatives should be performed without replacing the accelerations by the equations of motion, namely
\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}=2\sum_{A}m_{A}(3v_{A}^{\langle i}a_{A}^{j\rangle}+y_{A}^{\langle i}b_{A}^{j\rangle})
. One can then vary (
7b
) with respect to the first particle, namely
\bm{y}_{1}(t)\rightarrow\bm{y}_{1}(t)+\delta\bm{y}_{1}(t)
,
\bm{v}_{1}(t)\rightarrow\bm{v}_{1}(t)+\delta\bm{v}_{1}(t)
,
\bm{a}_{1}(t)\rightarrow\bm{a}_{1}(t)+\delta\bm{a}_{1}(t)
, etc.; using the identity of Footnote
1
, it follows that

\displaystyle\delta S_{\text{tail}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2G^{2}\mathrm{M}}{5c^{8}}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}t\ \Biggl\{-\frac{\delta r_{12}(t)}{r_{12}(t)}\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}(t)\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}(t)-2\ \delta\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}\!(t)\,\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}(t)\ln\left(\!\frac{r_{12}(t)}{b_{0}}\!\right)+\delta\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}\!(t)\ \mathrm{Pf}_{\frac{2b_{0}}{c}}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}}{|t-t^{\prime}|}\,\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}(t^{\prime})\Biggr\}

\displaystyle=\frac{2G^{2}\mathrm{M}}{5c^{8}}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}t\ \Biggl\{-\frac{\delta r_{12}(t)}{r_{12}(t)}\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}(t)\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}(t)+2\,\delta\mathrm{I}_{ij}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}}{\mathrm{d}t^{3}}\bigg[\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}(t)\ln\left(\frac{r_{12}(t)}{b_{0}}\right)\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\delta\mathrm{I}_{ij}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}\tau\,\ln\left(\frac{c\tau}{2b_{0}}\right)\bigg[\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(7)}(t-\tau)-\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(7)}(t+\tau)\bigg]\Biggr\}\,,

(8a)

where the second line has been obtained by integrating by parts. Now, using
\delta r_{12}=\delta y_{1}^{k}n_{12}^{k}
and
\delta\mathrm{I}_{ij}=2m_{1}\delta y_{1}^{\langle i}y_{1}^{j\rangle}
, the variation of the action can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\delta S_{\text{tail}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2G^{2}\mathrm{M}\,m_{1}}{5c^{8}}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}t\ \delta y_{1}^{i}\Biggl\{-\frac{n_{12}^{i}}{m_{1}\,r_{12}}\mathrm{I}_{jk}^{(3)}(t)\,\mathrm{I}_{jk}^{(3)}(t)+4y_{1}^{j}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}}{\mathrm{d}t^{3}}\bigg[\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}(t)\ln\left(\frac{r_{12}(t)}{b_{0}}\right)\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-2y_{1}^{j}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}\tau\,\ln\left(\frac{c\tau}{2b_{0}}\right)\bigg[\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(7)}(t-\tau)-\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(7)}(t+\tau)\bigg]\Biggr\}\,.

(8b)

One recovers the equations of motion by asking that
\delta S=\delta S_{\text{loc}}+\delta S_{\text{tail}}=0
. Recall that

\displaystyle S_{\text{loc}}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\bigg(\frac{m_{1}v_{1}^{2}}{2}+\frac{m_{2}v_{2}^{2}}{2}+\frac{Gm_{1}m_{2}}{r_{12}}\bigg)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{c^{2}}\right)

(9)

and that its variation (with respect to particle
1
) reads

\displaystyle\delta S_{\text{loc}}=-m_{1}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\delta y_{1}^{i}\left(a_{1}^{i}+\frac{Gm_{2}\,n_{12}^{i}}{r_{12}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{c^{2}}\right)\,;

(10)

thus, the expression (
5a
) for
\bm{a}_{1,\text{tail}}^{\text{cons}}
is exactly recovered from (
10
) and (
8
)

II.2
Formalism for constructing the 4PN Hamiltonian and treatment of the hereditary piece

The goal is now to construct a Hamiltonian from the action (
7
).
Recall that initially, the derivatives of the quadrupole moment entering the tail action (
7b
) should be performed without replacing the accelerations by the equations of motion. However, one can subsequently perform a coordinate shift, given explicitly in (5.15) of
12
; the action in the new coordinates can then be obtained by ‘naively’ using the order-reduced expression, given in (5.13) of
12
. For convenience, a coordinate transformation to the center-of-mass frame is then performed (this procedure can introduce extra non-localities at 4.5PN order
26
), in which case the equations of motion can be written in terms of the relative position
\bm{x}=\bm{y}_{1}-\bm{y}_{2}
, velocity
\bm{v}=\bm{v}_{1}-\bm{v}_{2}
and acceleration
\bm{a}=\bm{a}_{1}-\bm{a}_{2}
; the relative position vector is decomposed as
r=|\bm{x}|
and
\bm{n}=\bm{x}/r
.
The Hamiltonian is then extracted from this action by writing
12
;
14

\displaystyle S=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}t\Biggl[\bm{p}(t)\cdot\bm{x}(t)-H^{\text{loc}}(\bm{x}(t),\bm{p}(t))-H^{\text{tail}}[\bm{x},\bm{p}]\Biggr]\,,

(11)

where one immediately identifies
H^{\text{loc}}(\bm{x},\bm{p})=\bm{p}\cdot\bm{x}-L_{\text{loc}}(\bm{x},\bm{v}(\bm{x},\bm{p}))
and

\displaystyle H^{\text{tail}}[\bm{x},\bm{p}]=-\frac{G^{2}\mathrm{M}}{5c^{8}}\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}(t)\mathcal{T}_{ij}^{(3)}\,.

(12)

For any
N\in\mathbb{N}
, I have introduced
29

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{ij}^{(N)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pf}_{\frac{2r(t)}{c}}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}}{|t-t^{\prime}|}\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(N)}(t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-2\,\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(N)}\ln\left(\frac{r(t)}{b_{0}}\right)+\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\tau\ln\left(\frac{c\tau}{2b_{0}}\right)\Big[\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(N+1)}(t-\tau)-\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(N+1)}(t+\tau)\Big]\,,

(13)

where the order-reduced derivatives of the quadrupole moment were computed at leading order in (3.4) of
14
and read (after order reduction)

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2Gm}{r^{2}}\left(3p_{r}n^{\langle i}n^{j\rangle}-4n^{\langle i}p^{j\rangle}\right)\,,

(14a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(4)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2G}{r^{3}\nu}\Biggl\{\left[3\bm{p}^{2}-15p_{r}^{2}+\frac{Gm^{3}\nu^{2}}{r}\right]n^{\langle i}n^{j\rangle}+18p_{r}n^{\langle i}p^{j\rangle}-4p^{\langle i}p^{j\rangle}\Biggr\}\,.

(14b)

In order to obtain (the generalization of) Hamilton’s equations, one should again vary the action, but this time with respect to both
\bm{x}
and
\bm{p}
. This leads to the following equations (in polar coordinates):
2
2

2

In many works of the PN literature
14
;
29
;
28
, the factor
2
appearing in (
II.2
) is conventionally moved into the definition of the variation formula (
II.2
). Here, I choose conventions which agree with
67
;
58
;
68
;
32
;
31
;
33
;
77
; see Footnote
6
.

\displaystyle\dot{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial H^{\text{loc}}}{\partial p_{r}}+2\ \frac{\delta H^{\mathrm{tail}}}{\delta p_{r}(t)}\,,

\displaystyle\dot{\phi}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial H^{\text{loc}}}{\partial p_{\phi}}+2\ \frac{\delta H^{\mathrm{tail}}}{\delta p_{\phi}(t)}\,,

\displaystyle\dot{p}_{r}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\partial H^{\text{loc}}}{\mathcal{\partial}r}-2\ \frac{\mathcal{\delta H^{\text{tail}}}}{\mathcal{\delta}r(t)}\,,

\displaystyle\dot{p}_{\phi}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\partial H^{\text{loc}}}{\partial\phi}-2\ \frac{\mathcal{\delta H^{\text{tail}}}}{\mathcal{\delta}\phi(t)}\,,

(15)

where the notation
\delta/\delta r(t)
indicates that the differentiation is with respect to
r(t)
but not
r(t^{\prime})
(and similarly for
\phi
,
p_{r}
, and
p_{\phi}
); this arises from the doubling of the phase-space in such pseudo-Hamiltonian systems, or equivalently, from the
t\leftrightarrow t^{\prime}
symmetry of the tail action; see, e.g., (11) of
33
. The variational formulas then read
2

\displaystyle\frac{\delta H^{\text{tail}}}{\delta p_{r}(t)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{G^{2}\mathrm{M}}{5c^{8}}\frac{\partial I_{ij}^{(3)}}{\partial p_{r}}\mathcal{T}_{ij}^{(3)}\,,

\displaystyle\frac{\delta H^{\text{tail}}}{\delta p_{\phi}(t)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{G^{2}\mathrm{M}}{5c^{8}}\frac{\partial I_{ij}^{(3)}}{\partial p_{\phi}}\mathcal{T}_{ij}^{(3)}\,,

\displaystyle\frac{\delta H^{\text{tail}}}{\delta r(t)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{G^{2}\mathrm{M}}{5c^{8}}\Bigl[\frac{\partial I_{ij}^{(3)}}{\partial r}\mathcal{T}_{ij}^{(3)}-\frac{1}{r}\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}\Bigr]\,,

\displaystyle\frac{\delta H^{\text{tail}}}{\delta\phi(t)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{G^{2}\mathrm{M}}{5c^{8}}\frac{\partial I_{ij}^{(3)}}{\partial\phi}\mathcal{T}_{ij}^{(3)}\,.

(16)

It is worth stressing that the explicit
t\leftrightarrow t^{\prime}
symmetry of the action (when formulating it with a
partie finie
) leads to dynamical equations that do not involve the variation of the nonlocal tail term
\mathcal{T}_{ij}^{(3)}
, which greatly simplifies the study of these systems; the resulting equations of motion are still integro-differential, though.
This symmetry is actually always present in
pseudo-Hamiltonian
systems in the sense of Refs.
67
;
58
;
68
;
32
;
31
;
33
;
77
, and plays an important role in their study.

The motivation for constructing a Hamiltonian is to find constants of motion, which are conserved under the conservative dynamics. Indeed, if one restricts to the instantaneous (order-reduced) Hamiltonian, one has the usual property

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}H^{\text{loc}}_{\text{on-shell}}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Bigg|_{\bm{a}_{\text{cons}}^{\text{loc}}}=0

and

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}p_{\phi}^{\text{on-shell}}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Bigg|_{\bm{a}_{\text{cons}}^{\text{loc}}}=0\,,

(17)

where the Hamiltonian and angular momentum are evaluated after solving for the
instantaneous
, conservative equations of motion, and the time derivative is taken using this same acceleration. However, when instead taking the total time-derivative of the on-shell value of the
total, hereditary
Hamiltonian (where this time the full conservative equations of motion are used, including the hereditary term), this conservation property is lost, and one instead finds [see (3.8) and (3.18) of
14
]

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}H_{\text{on-shell}}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Bigg|_{\bm{a}_{\text{cons}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{G^{2}\mathrm{M}}{5c^{8}}\Biggl\{\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(4)}(t)\mathrm{Pf}_{\frac{2r(t)}{c}}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau}{|\tau|}\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}(t+\tau)-\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}(t)\mathrm{Pf}_{\frac{2r(t)}{c}}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau}{|\tau|}\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(4)}(t+\tau)\Biggr\}\,,

(18a)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}p_{\phi}^{\text{on-shell}}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Bigg|_{\bm{a}_{\text{cons}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4G^{2}\mathrm{M}}{5c^{8}}\varepsilon_{zjk}\mathrm{I}_{jl}^{(3)}\mathrm{Pf}_{\frac{2r(t)}{c}}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau}{|\tau|}\mathrm{I}_{kl}^{(3)}(t+\tau)\,,

(18b)

where the first index in the Levi-Civita symbol is the Cartesian
z
-component, which is orthogonal to the orbital plane.
The goal is thus to
localize
the Hamiltonian: the hereditary Hamiltonian should be transformed into an ordinary local Hamiltonian, such that the conserved energy is given in the usual manner by its on-shell value. This procedure is subtle, but it is now well understood, and can be addressed in different ways.

One way of looking at the problem, advocated by Refs.
47
;
48
, is to notice that higher-order Hamiltonians, which include a finite number of derivatives of the momenta, are not conserved on shell either. Such higher-order Hamiltonians have been studied in the context of the (local) 2PN and 3PN equations of motion
97
;
50
;
49
;
45
;
48
. They give rise to generalized Hamiltonian equations, which are expressed in terms of functional (rather than ordinary) derivatives.
A priori
, it is not allowed to replace the derivatives of the momenta inside the Hamiltonian using the equations of motion: such a ‘naively order-reduced’ Hamiltonian would give rise to incorrect equations of motion. The way to address the problem is then to go back to the action (or Lagrangian) and notice that there exists a point transformation such that the action becomes ordinary (i.e., without higher-order derivatives) when expressed in terms of the new variables
50
. This translates to a contact transformation on the phase-space variables, such that the Hamiltonian in the new variables is ordinary, and the energy corresponds to its on-shell value. Crucially, it was shown that the functional expression of the ordinary Hamiltonian in terms of the new phase-space variables is simply given by ‘naively’ order-reducing the higher-order Hamiltonian, with the additional information that the resulting reduced Hamiltonian should be interpreted as a function of the new variables.
One can then address nonlocal actions by Taylor-expanding the non-locality, effectively transforming the nonlocal action into a local action, which formally contains an infinite tower of derivatives of the coordinates. It can then be argued that the order-reduction procedure described previously carries through for an infinite tower of derivatives of the variables, which gives rise to a contact transformation which is
a priori
very complicated. However, it does not need to be controlled, since through the same argument, one concludes that the localized Hamiltonian in terms of the new variables is given by ‘naively’ localizing the Hamiltonian, with the additional information that the resulting localized Hamiltonian should be interpreted as a function of the new variables.
To illustrate this procedure more precisely, I introduce the toy action

\displaystyle S_{\text{toy}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\Bigg[\dot{x}^{2}+\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\tau\ G\big(x(t),x(t-\tau),x(t+\tau)\big)\Bigg]\,,

(19a)

where
G
is some arbitrary function. One could derive from this action some nonlocal equations of motion, like in Sec.
II.1
, but here I will do something else. Deliberately forgetting about convergence problems, e.g. by assuming that they can be dealt with by a regulating kernel
\mu(\tau)
inside the integral
48
, one performs a Taylor expansion around
\tau=0
and find

\displaystyle S_{\text{toy}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\Bigg[\dot{x}^{2}+\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\tau\ \widehat{G}\big[x(t),\dot{x}(t),\ddot{x}(t),\ldots;\tau\big]\Bigg]\,.

(19b)

Note that the explicit expression of
\widehat{G}
is also very complicated, and I do not attempt to control it explicitly here.
I then introduce

\displaystyle\widehat{F}\big[x(t),\dot{x}(t),\ddot{x}(t),\ldots\big]=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\tau\ \widehat{G}\big[x(t),\dot{x}(t),\ddot{x}(t),\ldots;\tau\big]

(20)

and find that its variation reads

\displaystyle\delta\widehat{F}

\displaystyle=\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}\frac{\partial\widehat{F}}{\partial{x}^{(N)}}\delta x^{(N)}=\delta x\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(-)^{N}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{N}}{\mathrm{d}t^{N}}\bigg[\frac{\partial\widehat{F}}{\partial x^{(N)}}\bigg]+\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Bigg\{\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\sum_{M=0}^{N-1}(-)^{M}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{M}}{\mathrm{d}t^{M}}\bigg[\frac{\partial\widehat{F}}{\partial x^{(N)}}\bigg]\delta x^{(N-1-M)}\Bigg\}\,,

(21)

where the
N
-th time derivative of
x(t)
is denoted as
x^{(N)}
.
I then vary the action (
19b
), use
the identity (
21
), integrate by parts, discard boundary terms associated with total derivatives, and finally find that the (formally) localized equations of motion read

\displaystyle\ddot{x}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}(-)^{N}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{N}}{\mathrm{d}t^{N}}\bigg[\frac{\partial\widehat{F}}{\partial x^{(N)}}\bigg]\,,

(22)

where the right-hand side depends on
x
,
\dot{x}
,
\ddot{x}
,
\dddot{x}
, etc. These equations of motion could have been equivalently obtained by Taylor expanding the nonlocal equations of motion obtained like in Sec.
II.1
. Due to this structure, these localized equations of motion are unusable in practice, and it is not guaranteed that the infinite sum converges. However, in formal analogy to the case of a
finite
number of higher-order derivatives, one postulates the existence of a point transformation that removes all higher-order derivatives in the action (
19b
). Following this analogy, one concludes that the associated ‘naively order-reduced’ hereditary Hamiltonian is a correct description of the motion in some new phase-space variables. However, one does not easily control in this way the transformation between the old and new phase-space variables; but as we will see, this is not necessary if one is only interested in linking the fundamental frequencies to the energy and angular momentum.

Another way of looking at the problem, advocated by Refs.
67
;
58
;
68
;
32
;
31
;
33
;
77
, is to realize that the hereditary action gives rise to a pseudo-Hamiltonian system. This means that the phase-space variables are doubled in the right-hand side of the associated Hamilton’s equations; one then takes derivatives with respect to only one of the variables, and only at the end are the doubled phase-space variables set to coincide. One then finds that the ‘naively order-reduced’ Hamiltonian leads to the correct equations of motion
in the old variables
, but for a
perturbed, noncanonical symplectic form
. This perturbation to the symplectic form was worked out explicitly. One can then perform an explicitly prescribed
noncanonical
transformation of the phase-space variables, such that in these
new variables
, the equations of motion are driven by the ‘naively order-reduced’ Hamiltonian and a canonical symplectic form. At the end of the day, this second approach leads to the exact same prescription for the localized Hamiltonian as that of Ref.
47
;
48
, but avoids the formal Taylor expansion and now also allows for an explicit control of the associated transformation of the phase-space variables, which could be useful for the construction of a 4PN quasi-Keplerian parametrization of the motion in ADM or modified harmonic coordinates (generalizing the local parametrization of
39
). I have explicitly applied this approach in Appendix
A
.

Finally, note that one can also construct conserved quantities by directly evaluating the right-hand sides of (
18
). From these, one then obtains corrections
\delta H
and
\delta p_{\phi}
which should be added to the on-shell values to obtain the truly conserved energy
E=H_{\text{on-shell}}+\delta H
and angular momentum
J=p_{\phi}^{\text{on-shell}}+\delta p_{\phi}
. This method was employed in Sec. IV of Ref.
14
, and was explicitly shown to yield identical results for the conserved quantities in the case of circular orbits; see also
29
. Due to practical considerations, this is not the method that will be employed here, but I would of course expect it to yield identical results.

II.3
Obtaining the local equations of motion from the local Hamiltonian

I will now focus on the treatment of the
local
equations of motion and conserved quantities at 4PN.
The local piece of the Hamiltonian (in ADM coordinates) is given to the 4PN order in Eqs. (5.13) and (5.17) of Ref.
46
in terms of rescaled variables. Namely, one should do the following replacements when going from the notations of that reference (which we refer to by the acronym ‘DJS’: Damour, Jaranowski and Schäfer) to my notations:

\displaystyle r_{\text{DJS}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\frac{r}{Gm}\,,

\displaystyle\bm{p}_{\text{DJS}}^{2}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\frac{p_{r}^{2}}{\mu}+\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{\mu r^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle(\bm{n}\cdot\bm{p})_{\text{DJS}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\frac{p_{r}}{\mu}\,.

(23)

The local Hamiltonian in terms of
(r,\phi,p_{r},p_{\phi})
is given by

H^{\text{loc}}=\frac{p_{r}^{2}}{2m\nu}+\frac{p_{\phi}^{2}}{2m\nu\,r^{2}}-\frac{Gm^{2}\nu}{r}+\cdots

(24)

where the higher-order terms are given in Ref.
46
in terms of rescaled variables; see also the Supplemental Material
2
for the complete expression.
One then solves for
({p}_{r},{p}_{\phi})
in the system of equations

\left\{\begin{aligned} \dot{r}&=\left[\frac{\partial H^{\text{loc}}}{\partial p_{r}}\right](p_{r},p_{\phi},r)\\
\dot{\phi}&=\left[\frac{\partial H^{\text{loc}}}{\partial p_{\phi}}\right](p_{r},p_{\phi},r)\end{aligned}\quad.\right.

(25)

This is done iteratively, order by order in the PN expansion. One finds that, on shell, these relations read

\displaystyle p_{r}^{\text{loc}}

\displaystyle=m\nu\dot{r}\Biggl\{1+\frac{1}{c^{2}}\Biggl[\frac{\dot{r}^{2}+r^{2}\dot{\phi}^{2}}{2}\left(1-3\nu\right)+\frac{Gm}{r}(3+\nu)\Biggr]+\dots\Biggr\}\,,

(26a)

\displaystyle p_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}

\displaystyle=m\nu r^{2}\dot{\phi}\Biggl\{1+\frac{1}{c^{2}}\Biggl[\frac{\dot{r}^{2}+r^{2}\dot{\phi}^{2}}{2}\left(1-3\nu\right)+\frac{Gm}{r}(3+\nu)\Biggr]+\cdots\Biggr\}\,,

(26b)

where the complete local 4PN expressions have been relegated to the Supplemental Material
2
.
The (local) energy and angular momentum are then obtained, respectively, as the on-shell value of the (local) Hamiltonian and the momentum conjugate to the angle
\phi
, namely

\displaystyle E^{\text{loc}}

\displaystyle=H^{\text{loc}}\left[r,p_{r}^{\text{loc}}(\dot{r},\dot{\phi},r),p_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}(\dot{r},\dot{\phi},r)\right]\,,

(27a)

\displaystyle J^{\text{loc}}

\displaystyle=p_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}(\dot{r},\dot{\phi},r)\,.

(27b)

Using the expressions for the conjugate momenta (
26
) and appropriately PN-expanding, I find that

\displaystyle E^{\text{loc}}

\displaystyle=m\nu\,\Biggl\{\frac{\dot{r}^{2}}{2}+\frac{r^{2}\dot{\phi}^{2}}{2}-\frac{Gm}{r}+\cdots\Biggr\}\,,

(28a)

\displaystyle J^{\text{loc}}

\displaystyle=m\nu r^{2}\dot{\phi}\,\Biggl\{1+\frac{1}{c^{2}}\Biggl[\frac{\dot{r}^{2}+r^{2}\dot{\phi}^{2}}{2}\left(1-3\nu\right)+\frac{Gm}{r}(3+\nu)\Biggr]+\cdots\Biggr\}\,,

(28b)

where the complete local 4PN expressions have been relegated to the Supplemental Material
2
. Note that these expressions differ from Eqs. (3.1) and (3.2) of Ref.
15
, simply because the latter reference works in harmonic coordinates rather than ADM coordinates.
Using the fact that
(E^{\text{loc}},J^{\text{loc}})
are constants of motion (for the
local
equations of motion), one can finally solve for
\{\dot{r}^{2},\dot{\phi}\}
in the previous expression (
28
). They have a polynomial structure in
s=1/r
,

\displaystyle\big[\dot{r}^{2}\big]^{\text{loc}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}(1/r)

and

\displaystyle\big[\dot{\phi}\big]^{\text{loc}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}(1/r)\,,

(29)

where the polynomials read

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(s)

\displaystyle=A+2Bs+Cs^{2}+D_{1}s^{3}+D_{2}s^{4}+D_{3}s^{5}+D_{4}s^{6}+D_{5}s^{7}+D_{6}s^{8}+D_{7}s^{9}+\mathcal{O}(10)\,,

(30a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(s)

\displaystyle=Fs^{2}+I_{1}s^{3}+I_{2}s^{4}+I_{3}s^{5}+I_{4}s^{6}+I_{5}s^{7}+I_{6}s^{8}+I_{7}s^{9}+\mathcal{O}(10)\,,

(30b)

in which ignorable terms that are 5PN or higher are denoted by
\mathcal{O}(10)
.
The coefficients
(A,B,C,D_{n},F,I_{n})
are functions of energy and angular momentum, and I provide them in Eq. (
157
) (see also the Supplemental Material
2
) in terms of the reduced energy and angular momentum

\displaystyle\varepsilon=-\frac{2E}{m\nu c^{2}}

and

\displaystyle j=-\frac{2J^{2}E}{G^{2}m^{5}\nu^{3}}\,,

(31)

which are defined such that
\varepsilon=\mathcal{O}(1/c^{2})
and
j=\mathcal{O}(1)
. They have the following PN scalings:
A
,
B
,
C
, and
F
are of Newtonian order
\sim\mathcal{O}(1)
;
D_{1}
and
I_{1}
are of 1PN order
\sim\mathcal{O}(1/c^{2})
;
D_{2}
,
I_{2}
,
D_{3}
, and
I_{3}
are of 2PN order
\sim\mathcal{O}(1/c^{4})
;
D_{4}
,
I_{4}
,
D_{5}
, and
I_{5}
are of 3PN order
\sim\mathcal{O}(1/c^{6})
; and
D_{6}
,
I_{6}
,
D_{7}
, and
I_{7}
are of 4PN order
\sim\mathcal{O}(1/c^{8})
. I find perfect agreement for these coefficients with Ref.
39
; see Eqs. (9) and (16) in that reference, as well as the associated Supplemental Material. I also agree with the 3PN result of Eq. (A1) of Ref.
81
for
A
,
B
,
C
,
D_{n}
.
Finally, injecting (
29
) into (
26a
), I find that
p_{r}^{2}
can be expressed (on shell) as a polynomial in
r
, namely

\displaystyle\big[p_{r}^{2}\big]^{\text{loc}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}(1/r)\,,

(32)

where the polynomial reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(s)=\mathcal{A}+2\mathcal{B}s+\mathcal{C}s^{2}+\mathcal{D}_{1}s^{3}+\mathcal{D}_{2}s^{4}+\mathcal{D}_{3}s^{5}+\mathcal{D}_{4}s^{6}+\mathcal{D}_{5}s^{7}+\mathcal{D}_{6}s^{8}+\mathcal{D}_{7}s^{9}+\mathcal{O}(10)

(33)

and where the coefficients are given in terms of
(\varepsilon,j)
in Eq. (
156
); see also the Supplemental Material
2
. They are in agreement
3
3

3

To find agreement, once must first specify the values
\omega_{\text{static}}=0
and
\omega_{\text{kinetic}}=41/24
, which were determined after the publication of Ref.
45
. Moreover, note the following typo in Eq. (B1f) of Ref.
45
: the first term of that equation (i.e., the 2PN piece) should be
\frac{1}{c^{4}}\frac{3}{4}\nu^{2}j^{4}
; compare to the correct 2PN-accurate expression in Eq. (3.4f) of Ref.
44
.
at 3PN with Eq. (B1) of Ref.
45
. They have the following PN scalings:
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
, and
\mathcal{C}
are of Newtonian order
\sim\mathcal{O}(1)
;
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is of 1PN order
\sim\mathcal{O}(1/c^{2})
;
\mathcal{D}_{2}
and
\mathcal{D}_{3}
are of 2PN order
\sim\mathcal{O}(1/c^{4})
;
\mathcal{D}_{4}
and
\mathcal{D}_{5}
are of 3PN order
\sim\mathcal{O}(1/c^{6})
; and
\mathcal{D}_{6}
and
\mathcal{D}_{7}
are of 4N order
\sim\mathcal{O}(1/c^{8})
. Note that these coefficients, in calligraphic script, differ from those in italic script of Eq. (
157
).

III
Action-angle formulation of the local dynamics

In this section, I will obtain the local Hamiltonian in terms of action variables. In the process, I obtain the expressions for the (local) action variables in terms of the conserved (local) energy and angular momentum, which are themselves expressed in terms of the original coordinates. To avoid clutter, I will often omit the ‘loc’ label on the right-hand side of such relations.

III.1
Action variables in the local sector

I will assume that the reader is familiar with the Hamilton-Jacobi formalism applied to the Kepler problem; see, e.g., Sec. 10.8 of Ref.
60
. In the absence of spin effects, the local, 4PN motion is fully separable in spherical coordinates, which makes it straightforward to define the usual set of action variables (with dimensions
[\text{mass}]\times[\text{length}]^{2}\times[\text{time}]^{-1}
):

\displaystyle I_{r}^{\text{loc}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\oint\mathrm{d}r\,p_{r}^{\text{loc}}

\displaystyle\,,

\displaystyle I_{\theta}^{\text{loc}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\oint\mathrm{d}\theta\,p_{\theta}^{\text{loc}}

\displaystyle\,,

\displaystyle I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\oint\mathrm{d}\phi\,p_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}\,.

(34)

One then switches to the Delaunay variables
51
;
52
to better address the degeneracies of the Kepler problem; these read, in Goldstein’s notation
60

\displaystyle I_{1}^{\text{loc}}=I_{\theta}^{\text{loc}}

\displaystyle\,,

\displaystyle I_{2}^{\text{loc}}=I_{\theta}^{\text{loc}}+I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}

\displaystyle\,,

\displaystyle I_{3}^{\text{loc}}=I_{r}^{\text{loc}}+I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}+I_{\theta}^{\text{loc}}\,,

(35)

which are such that the Newtonian Hamiltonian depends only on the action variable
I_{3}^{\text{loc}}
.
Since spins are neglected, the motion is planar:
I_{\theta}^{\text{loc}}=0
and
\theta=\pi/2
. Restricting to the orbital plane, the action-angle formulation of the motion only involves two pairs of dynamical action-variables
18
,

\displaystyle I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}

\displaystyle=I_{2}^{\text{loc}}

and

\displaystyle I_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}}

\displaystyle=I_{r}^{\text{loc}}+I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}=I_{3}^{\text{loc}}\,.

(36)

Note that
I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}=p_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}=J^{\text{loc}}
is simply the angular momentum. The only non-trivial quantity to compute in terms of energy and angular momentum is the (local) radial action. After changing variables from
r
to
s=1/r
for convenience, it follows that the (local) radial action can be rewritten using Eqs. (
34
) and (
32
) as

\displaystyle I_{r}^{\text{loc}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\oint\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s^{2}}\sqrt{\mathcal{I}(s)}\,.

(37)

Thanks to Eq. (
33
), this can be computed
à la
Sommerfeld using complex analysis
100
;
60
;
44
. First, perform (under the integral sign) the PN expansion
\sqrt{\mathcal{I}(s)}=\sqrt{\mathcal{A}+2\mathcal{B}s+\mathcal{C}s^{2}}\sum_{k}\alpha_{k}s^{k}
, where the coefficients
\alpha_{k}
depend on the coefficients
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
, etc., and can be straightforwardly determined from the PN expansion. One is then left with the computation of the following master integrals:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{p,q}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\oint\mathrm{d}s\,s^{p-2}\left(\mathcal{A}+2\mathcal{B}s+\mathcal{C}s^{2}\right)^{1/2-q},

(38)

where
(p,q)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
; recall that
\mathcal{A}<0
,
\mathcal{B}>0
, and
\mathcal{C}<0
for bound orbits. Using the residue theorem, one finally finds that
103

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{p,q}

\displaystyle=\big[p=0\big](-1)^{q}(1-2q)\mathcal{B}(-\mathcal{A})^{-1/2-q}+\big[p=1\big](-1)^{q+1}(-\mathcal{A})^{1/2-q}

\displaystyle+\big[p\geq 2q\big](-1)^{q+1}(-\mathcal{C})^{1/2-q}\sum_{k=\lceil\frac{p}{2}-q\rceil}^{p-2q}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}-q+1\right)\mathcal{A}^{p-k-2q}\,(2\mathcal{B})^{2k+2q-p}\,\mathcal{C}^{-k}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}-q-k+1\right)(2k+2q-p)!(p-k-2q)!}\,,

(39)

where
\Gamma(x)
is the gamma function,
\lceil n\rceil
is the ceiling of the integer
n
, and
\big[\mathcal{P}\big]
is the Iverson bracket
108
;
69
defined such that
[\mathcal{P}]=1
if
\mathcal{P}
is true, and
[\mathcal{P}]=0
otherwise. This formula, which first appeared in Eq. (A4) of Ref.
103
, reproduces all the integrals obtained for particular values of
(p,q)
in Eq. (B2) of Ref.
45
. It would be interesting to extend this formula to the case where there are logarithmic terms in
r
, see Appendix C of
14
.
One then finds that the averaged radial action can be expressed at 4PN order in terms of the coefficients
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
, etc. as follows:

\displaystyle I_{r}^{\text{loc}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathcal{B}}{\sqrt{-\mathcal{A}}}-\sqrt{-\mathcal{C}}+\frac{\mathcal{B}\mathcal{D}_{1}}{2(-\mathcal{C})^{3/2}}+\frac{15\mathcal{B}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{1}^{2}-3\mathcal{A}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}_{1}^{2}-12\mathcal{B}^{2}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}_{2}+4\mathcal{A}\mathcal{C}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{2}+20\mathcal{B}^{3}\mathcal{D}_{3}-12\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}_{3}}{16(-\mathcal{C})^{7/2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{32(-\mathcal{C})^{11/2}}\biggl[105\mathcal{B}^{3}\mathcal{D}_{1}^{3}-35\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}_{1}^{3}-140\mathcal{B}^{3}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{2}+60\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{2}+315\mathcal{B}^{4}\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{3}-210\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}^{2}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{3}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+15\mathcal{A}^{2}\mathcal{C}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{3}-70\mathcal{B}^{4}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}_{4}+60\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}^{2}\mathcal{C}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{4}-6\mathcal{A}^{2}\mathcal{C}^{3}\mathcal{D}_{4}+126\mathcal{B}^{5}\mathcal{D}_{5}-140\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}^{3}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}_{5}+30\mathcal{A}^{2}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{5}\biggr]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{1024(-\mathcal{C})^{15/2}}\biggl[15015\mathcal{B}^{4}\mathcal{D}_{1}^{4}-6930\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}^{2}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}_{1}^{4}+315\mathcal{A}^{2}\mathcal{C}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{1}^{4}-27720\mathcal{B}^{4}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}_{1}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{2}+15120\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}^{2}\mathcal{C}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{1}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-840\mathcal{A}^{2}\mathcal{C}^{3}\mathcal{D}_{1}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{2}+5040\mathcal{B}^{4}\mathcal{C}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{2}^{2}-3360\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}^{2}\mathcal{C}^{3}\mathcal{D}_{2}^{2}+240\mathcal{A}^{2}\mathcal{C}^{4}\mathcal{D}_{2}^{2}+72072\mathcal{B}^{5}\mathcal{D}_{1}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{3}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-55440\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}^{3}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}_{1}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{3}+7560\mathcal{A}^{2}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{1}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{3}-22176\mathcal{B}^{5}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}_{2}\mathcal{D}_{3}+20160\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}^{3}\mathcal{C}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{2}\mathcal{D}_{3}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-3360\mathcal{A}^{2}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}^{3}\mathcal{D}_{2}\mathcal{D}_{3}+24024\mathcal{B}^{6}\mathcal{D}_{3}^{2}-27720\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}^{4}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}_{3}^{2}+7560\mathcal{A}^{2}\mathcal{B}^{2}\mathcal{C}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{3}^{2}-280\mathcal{A}^{3}\mathcal{C}^{3}\mathcal{D}_{3}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-22176\mathcal{B}^{5}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{4}+20160\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}^{3}\mathcal{C}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{4}-3360\mathcal{A}^{2}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}^{3}\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{4}+48048\mathcal{B}^{6}\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{5}-55440\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}^{4}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{5}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+15120\mathcal{A}^{2}\mathcal{B}^{2}\mathcal{C}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{5}-560\mathcal{A}^{3}\mathcal{C}^{3}\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{5}-7392\mathcal{B}^{6}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}_{6}+10080\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}^{4}\mathcal{C}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{6}-3360\mathcal{A}^{2}\mathcal{B}^{2}\mathcal{C}^{3}\mathcal{D}_{6}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+160\mathcal{A}^{3}\mathcal{C}^{4}\mathcal{D}_{6}+13728\mathcal{B}^{7}\mathcal{D}_{7}-22176\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}^{5}\mathcal{C}\mathcal{D}_{7}+10080\mathcal{A}^{2}\mathcal{B}^{3}\mathcal{C}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{7}-1120\mathcal{A}^{3}\mathcal{B}\mathcal{C}^{3}\mathcal{D}_{7}\biggr]\,.

(40)

It will now prove useful to introduce the rescaled action variables (with dimensions
[\text{length}]^{-1}[\text{time}]
)

\displaystyle i_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}=\frac{I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}}{Gm^{2}\nu}

\displaystyle\,,

\displaystyle i_{r}^{\text{loc}}=\frac{I_{r}^{\text{loc}}}{Gm^{2}\nu}

\displaystyle\,,

\displaystyle i_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}}=\frac{I_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}}}{Gm^{2}\nu}\,,

(41)

where
I_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}}=I_{r}^{\text{loc}}+I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}
. Note that
\{c\,i_{\phi}^{\text{loc}},c\,i_{r}^{\text{loc}},c\,i_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}}\}
are dimensionless quantities; the reduced action variables are given the dimension of an inverse velocity in order to keep
c
as the PN order counting parameter.
The expressions for the coefficients
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}_{n})
, provided in Eq. (
156
), are then plugged back into Eq. (
III.1
). This results in the following expression for the radial action at 4PN in terms of the (local) energy and angular momentum:

\displaystyle i_{r}^{\text{loc}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\Biggl\{1-\sqrt{j}+\varepsilon\Biggl[-\frac{15}{8}+\frac{\nu}{8}+\frac{3}{\sqrt{j}}\Biggr]+\varepsilon^{2}\Biggl[\frac{35}{128}+\frac{15}{64}\nu+\frac{3}{128}\nu^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{j}}\biggl(-\frac{15}{4}+\frac{3}{2}\nu\biggr)+\frac{1}{j^{3/2}}\biggl(\frac{35}{4}-\frac{5}{2}\nu\biggr)\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad+\varepsilon^{3}\Biggl[\frac{21}{1024}-\frac{105}{1024}\nu+\frac{15}{1024}\nu^{2}+\frac{5}{1024}\nu^{3}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{j}}\biggl(\frac{15}{16}-\frac{15}{16}\nu+\frac{3}{4}\nu^{2}\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{j^{3/2}}\biggl(-\frac{105}{4}+\nu\Bigl(\frac{109}{3}-\frac{41}{128}\pi^{2}\Bigr)-\frac{15}{4}\nu^{2}\biggr)+\frac{1}{j^{5/2}}\biggl(\frac{231}{4}+\nu\left(-\frac{125}{2}+\frac{123}{128}\pi^{2}\right)+\frac{21}{8}\nu^{2}\biggr)\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad+\varepsilon^{4}\Biggl[\frac{99}{32768}-\frac{105}{8192}\nu+\frac{105}{16384}\nu^{2}+\frac{15}{8192}\nu^{3}+\frac{35}{32768}\nu^{4}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{j}}\biggl(-\frac{15}{32}\nu^{2}+\frac{3}{8}\nu^{3}\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{j^{3/2}}\biggl(\frac{1575}{64}+\nu\Bigl(-\frac{20323}{288}+\frac{35569}{24576}\pi^{2}\Bigr)+\nu^{2}\Bigl(\frac{4045}{96}-\frac{205}{512}\pi^{2}\Bigr)-\frac{15}{4}\nu^{3}\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{j^{7/2}}\biggl(\frac{32175}{64}+\nu\Bigl(-\frac{248057}{288}+\frac{425105}{24576}\pi^{2}\Bigr)+\nu^{2}\Bigl(\frac{18925}{96}-\frac{1025}{256}\pi^{2}\Bigr)-\frac{45}{16}\nu^{3}\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{j^{5/2}}\biggl(-\frac{9009}{32}+\nu\Bigl(\frac{293413}{480}-\frac{51439}{4096}\pi^{2}\Bigr)+\nu^{2}\Bigl(-\frac{7013}{32}+\frac{123}{32}\pi^{2}\Bigr)+\frac{105}{16}\nu^{3}\biggr)\Biggr]+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{5})\Biggr\}\,,

(42)

where I wrote
(\varepsilon,j)
instead of
(\varepsilon^{\text{loc}},j^{\text{loc}})
to avoid clutter. Up to the conventional factor
m\nu^{2}
, this expression is in agreement
4
4

4

In Eq. (4.2c) of that reference,
\nu^{4}
should in fact be
\nu^{3}
.
with
76
at 3PN order. The azimuthal action variable, by definition, is simply given by

\displaystyle i_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c}\sqrt{\frac{j}{\varepsilon}}\,.

(43)

The other Delaunay variable is then simply related to the radial action by

\displaystyle i_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}}

\displaystyle=i_{r}^{\text{loc}}+\frac{1}{c}\sqrt{\frac{j}{\varepsilon}}\,,

(44)

such that
i_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}}
differs from
i_{r}^{\text{loc}}
only by its Newtonian coefficient. The leading-order behavior of each action variable is thus

\displaystyle i_{r\phi}=\frac{1}{c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\Bigl(1+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)\Bigr)

and

\displaystyle i_{r}=\frac{1-\sqrt{j}}{c\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\Bigl(1+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)\Bigr)\,.

(45)

III.2
Local Hamiltonian in terms of action variables

In order to obtain the Hamiltonian in terms of the action variables, it suffices to solve (iteratively) for the energy
\varepsilon
in Eqs. (
III.1
) and (
44
); note that the angular momentum is trivially related to
i_{\phi}
by (
43
). At 4PN, the local Hamiltonian (or energy) reads

\displaystyle H^{\text{loc}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})

\displaystyle=-\frac{m\nu}{2i_{r\phi}^{\,2}}\Biggl\{1+\frac{1}{c^{2}}\Biggl[\frac{1}{i_{r\phi}^{\,2}}\biggl(-\frac{15}{4}+\frac{\nu}{4}\biggr)+\frac{6}{i_{\phi}i_{r\phi}}\Biggr]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{c^{4}}\Biggl[\frac{1}{i_{r\phi}^{\,4}}\biggl(\frac{145}{8}-\frac{15}{8}\nu+\frac{\nu^{2}}{8}\biggr)+\frac{1}{i_{\phi}i_{r\phi}^{\,3}}\biggl(-\frac{105}{2}+6\nu\biggr)+\frac{27}{i_{\phi}^{\,2}i_{r\phi}^{\,2}}+\frac{1}{i_{\phi}^{\,3}i_{r\phi}}\biggl(\frac{35}{2}-5\nu\biggr)\Biggr]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{c^{6}}\Biggl[\frac{1}{i_{r\phi}^{\,6}}\biggl(-\frac{6363}{64}+\frac{805}{64}\nu-\frac{45}{32}\nu^{2}+\frac{5}{64}\nu^{3}\biggr)+\frac{1}{i_{\phi}i_{r\phi}^{\,5}}\biggl(\frac{825}{2}-75\nu+6\nu^{2}\biggr)+\frac{1}{i_{\phi}^{\,2}i_{r\phi}^{\,4}}\biggl(-450+\frac{135}{2}\nu\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{i_{\phi}^{\,3}i_{r\phi}^{\,3}}\biggl(-\frac{303}{4}+\nu\left(\frac{1427}{12}-\frac{41}{64}\pi^{2}\right)-10\nu^{2}\biggr)+\frac{1}{i_{\phi}^{\,4}i_{r\phi}^{\,2}}\biggl(\frac{315}{2}-45\nu\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{i_{\phi}^{\,5}i_{r\phi}}\biggl(\frac{231}{2}+\nu\left(-125+\frac{123}{64}\pi^{2}\right)+\frac{21}{4}\nu^{2}\biggr)\Biggr]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{c^{8}}\Biggl[\frac{1}{i_{r\phi}^{\,8}}\biggl(\frac{75303}{128}-\frac{10713}{128}\nu+\frac{1545}{128}\nu^{2}-\frac{75}{64}\nu^{3}+\frac{7}{128}\nu^{4}\biggr)+\frac{1}{i_{\phi}i_{r\phi}^{\,7}}\biggl(-\frac{50703}{16}+\frac{5745}{8}\nu-\frac{195}{2}\nu^{2}+6\nu^{3}\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{i_{\phi}^{\,2}i_{r\phi}^{\,6}}\biggl(\frac{85365}{16}-\frac{10395}{8}\nu+\frac{945}{8}\nu^{2}\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{i_{\phi}^{\,3}i_{r\phi}^{\,5}}\biggl(-\frac{46275}{32}+\nu\left(-\frac{59639}{72}+\frac{124129}{12288}\pi^{2}\right)+\nu^{2}\left(\frac{1547}{6}-\frac{41}{32}\pi^{2}\right)-15\nu^{3}\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{i_{\phi}^{\,4}i_{r\phi}^{\,4}}\biggl(-\frac{21435}{8}+\nu\left(\frac{8035}{4}-\frac{615}{64}\pi^{2}\right)-\frac{375}{2}\nu^{2}\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{i_{\phi}^{\,5}i_{r\phi}^{\,3}}\biggl(-\frac{7749}{16}+\nu\left(\frac{467473}{240}-\frac{80959}{2048}\pi^{2}\right)+\nu^{2}\left(-\frac{8643}{16}+\frac{1107}{128}\pi^{2}\right)+\frac{63}{4}\nu^{3}\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{i_{\phi}^{\,6}i_{r\phi}^{\,2}}\biggl(\frac{20307}{16}+\nu\left(-\frac{5025}{4}+\frac{1107}{64}\pi^{2}\right)+66\nu^{2}\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{i_{\phi}^{\,7}i_{r\phi}}\biggl(\frac{32175}{32}+\nu\left(-\frac{248057}{144}+\frac{425105}{12288}\pi^{2}\right)+\nu^{2}\left(\frac{18925}{48}-\frac{1025}{128}\pi^{2}\right)-\frac{45}{8}\nu^{3}\biggr)\Biggr]+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{c^{10}}\right)\Biggr\}\,,

where again, I wrote
(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})
instead of
(i_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}},i_{\phi}^{\text{loc}})
to avoid clutter.
By construction, the Hamiltonian does not depend on the angle variables. This expression agrees with Eq. (3.13) of Ref.
44
at 2PN and with Eq. (4.18) of Ref.
45
at 3PN (once the correct ambiguity parameters are chosen, namely
\omega_{\mathrm{static}}=0
and
\omega_{\mathrm{kinetic}}=41/24
).

III.3
Angle variables in the local problem

Having constructed the (local)
action
variables
(I_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}},I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}})
, one now wants to determine the (local)
angle
variables
(\ell^{\text{loc}},g^{\text{loc}})
, with the requirement that they must be conjugate to the action variables and grow linearly in time, namely

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\ell^{\text{loc}}}{\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=n^{\text{loc}}=\frac{\partial H^{\text{loc}}}{\partial I_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}}}\bigg|_{I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}}=\frac{1}{Gm^{2}\nu}\frac{\partial H^{\text{loc}}}{\partial i_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}}}\bigg|_{i_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}}\,,

(47a)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}g^{\text{loc}}}{\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=\omega^{\text{loc}}-n^{\text{loc}}=\frac{\partial H^{\text{loc}}}{\partial I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}}\bigg|_{I_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}}}=\frac{1}{Gm^{2}\nu}\frac{\partial H^{\text{loc}}}{\partial i_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}}\bigg|_{i_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}}}\,.

(47b)

Here, I have introduced the (local) radial frequency
n^{\text{loc}}
and azimuthal frequency
\omega^{\text{loc}}
. Thus, the (local) action-angle variables are the standard Delaunay variables
5
5

5

Here, I follow the conventions of
47
, but one could also have alternatively worked with another combination of these variables introduced by Poincaré, which are better adapted to taking the limit of circular orbits:
(\lambda=\ell+g,\varpi=-g,I_{r\phi},I_{r})
; see Sec. IV of
48
.

of celestial mechanics:
\ell^{\text{loc}}
is the
mean anomaly
and
g^{\text{loc}}
is the
argument of the periastron
. Note that at Newtonian order, the problem is degenerate, since
\omega=n+\mathcal{O}(1/c^{2})
and
g
is a constant at this order (there is no periastron precession).

In order to construct the angle variables, one introduces the characteristic function
60

\displaystyle W^{\text{loc}}(r,\phi;I_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}},I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}})=I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}\,\phi+\int^{r}\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}\sqrt{\mathcal{I}(1/r^{\prime};I_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}},I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}})}\,,

(48)

which is the type-2 generating function for the transformation between
(r,\phi,p_{r}^{\text{loc}},p_{\phi}^{\text{loc}})
and the action-angle variables
(\ell^{\text{loc}},g^{\text{loc}},I_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}},I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}})
. Note that
\mathcal{I}(1/r^{\prime};I_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}},I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}})
was defined by Eq. (
33
), where
J^{\text{loc}}=I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}=p_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}
and where
E^{\text{loc}}
should be expressed in terms of the action variables using Eq. (
III.2
).
By construction of the action variables, one recovers
p_{r}^{\text{loc}}=\partial W^{\text{loc}}/\partial r
and
p_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}=\partial W^{\text{loc}}/\partial\phi
. The angle variables are then defined as
\ell^{\text{loc}}=\partial W^{\text{loc}}/\partial I_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}}
and
g=\partial W^{\text{loc}}/\partial I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}
, thus ensuring that they are conjugate to the action variables. The angle variables have thus been expressed in terms of
(r,\phi,I_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}},I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}})
, or equivalently, in terms of
(r,\phi,E^{\text{loc}},J^{\text{loc}})
; however, this expression is still plagued with an integral, preventing it from being entirely explicit. In the local sector, this was solved explicitly up to 4PN
39
;
81
;
98
;
43
; the solution is called the
quasi-Keplerian parametrization
, and is the 4PN generalization of the Keplerian parametrization of Sec.
IV.2
. Alternatively, one can invoke the periodic nature of the motion and expand it into a Fourier series, namely

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=\sum_{(p,q)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}{}_{p,q}\mathcal{C}_{r}(I_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}},I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}})\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p\,\ell^{\text{loc}}+q\,g^{\text{loc}})}\ ,

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle=\sum_{(p,q)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}{}_{p,q}\mathcal{C}_{\phi}(I_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}},I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}})\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p\,\ell^{\text{loc}}+q\,g^{\text{loc}})}\ ,

(49)

where
{}_{p,q}\mathcal{C}_{r}(I_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}},I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}})
and
{}_{p,q}\mathcal{C}_{\phi}(I_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}},I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}})
are some coefficients to be determined.

IV
Tail corrections to the local Hamiltonian in action-angle variables

The objective is now to study the full problem, including the tails. This will be made possible by the existence of action variables associated with the full Hamiltonian. Restricting to the motion in the orbit plane (
\theta=\pi/2
), these are expressed in terms of the one-form
\Theta=p_{r}\,\mathrm{d}r+p_{\phi}\,\mathrm{d}\phi
as
5
;
99
;
107

\displaystyle I_{k}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\oint_{\mathcal{C}_{k}}\Theta\,,

(50)

where
k\in\{r,\phi\}
and
\mathcal{C}_{k}
is a closed curve on the
2
-torus in the 4-dimensional phase space
(r,\phi,p_{r},p_{\phi})
defined by the equations of motion. For definiteness, suppose that
p_{r}
and
p_{\phi}
can be written as functions of
(r,\phi)
. The action variables are independent of the specific choice of curve; they only depend on the homotopy class of the curve, which is specified by the label
k\in\{r,\phi\}
. This fact makes action variables gauge-invariant and thus extremely useful objects. However, in the absence of separability, it is very difficult (or sometimes impossible) to compute these explicitly, unless they are obtained as a perturbation of a separable system. Thus, the tail contributions are treated as perturbations to the actions (
41
) associated with the local, separable problem. Just like in the local case, the motion is planar, so
p_{\theta}=0
and one can rotate the frame such that
\theta=0
; the
\theta
coordinate is thus ignorable. One then naturally introduces the Delaunay variable
I_{r\phi}=I_{r}+I_{\phi}
as well as the rescaled action variables

\displaystyle i_{\phi}=\frac{I_{\phi}}{Gm^{2}\nu}

\displaystyle\,,

\displaystyle i_{r}=\frac{I_{r}}{Gm^{2}\nu}

\displaystyle\,,

\displaystyle i_{r\phi}=\frac{I_{r\phi}}{Gm^{2}\nu}\,,

(51)

which will differ from their local counterparts (
41
) only by small 4PN contributions due to the tails. Note that
I_{\phi}
and
J
are always trivially related (
I_{\phi}=J
or
I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}=J^{\text{loc}}
), but that
I_{\phi}\neq I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}
.

I will closely follow the method laid out in Ref.
33
, which establishes precisely how to localize nonlocal perturbation to a local Hamiltonian. One key difference with the usual treatment of Chapter 14 of
60
is that the contact transformation from the unperturbed variables to the perturbed variables is not canonical anymore, due to the nonlocal nature of the perturbation. Thus, the local Delaunay variables that have been constructed are still valid variables to describe the problem, but they are not action-angle variables with respect to the perturbed Hamiltonian. They are not even canonical anymore: the perturbed system in these variables also acquires a perturbation to the (canonical) symplectic form.

I will now describe the various contact transformations of the phase-space variables to recover an action-angle formulation for the perturbed problem.

IV.1
Splitting the tail term into a logarithmic and hereditary contribution

The local action-angle variables that have been constructed (associated with the local Hamiltonian
H^{\text{loc}}
) will now be denoted by
(\ell^{\text{loc}},g^{\text{loc}},I_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}},I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}})
. However, it does not matter whether one uses the local or complete variables when working at leading order, so I will drop the ‘loc’ tag in that case. At Newtonian order, the Keplerian parameters are also introduced as functions of the action variables

\displaystyle a(I_{r\phi},I_{\phi})

\displaystyle=\frac{I_{r\phi}^{2}}{Gm^{3}\nu^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle e(I_{r\phi},I_{\phi})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{1-\frac{I_{\phi}^{2}}{I_{r\phi}^{2}}}\,.

(52)

The tail Hamiltonian is then split in to a ‘logarithmic’ and a ‘hereditary’ part:
H^{\text{tail}}=H^{\text{log}}+H^{\text{hered}}
. The individual pieces are defined as

\displaystyle H^{\text{log}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2G^{2}m}{5c^{8}}\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}\ln\left(\frac{r}{\eta}\right)\,,

(53a)

\displaystyle H^{\text{hered}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{G^{2}m}{5c^{8}}\ \mathrm{Pf}_{\frac{2\eta}{c}}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}}{|t-t^{\prime}|}\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}(t)\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}(t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-\frac{G^{2}m}{5c^{8}}\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}(t)\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\tau\ln\left(\frac{c\tau}{2\eta}\right)\left[\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(4)}(t-\tau)-\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(4)}(t+\tau)\right]\,,

(53b)

where I have introduced the scale

\displaystyle\eta=\frac{1}{4}\exp(-\gamma_\mathrm{E})\sqrt{\frac{c^{2}a^{3}}{Gm}}\,,

(54)

and where the ADM mass
M
has been replaced (at this order) by the total mass
m
.
The specific scale (
54
) was chosen to simplify the expression of the localized hereditary Hamiltonian, see Eq. (
68
).
The logarithmic Hamiltonian
H^{\text{log}}
is a non-hereditary perturbation to
H^{\text{loc}}
, and can be treated straightforwardly using time-independent canonical perturbation theory, as described in Section 12.4 of
60
; see also App. C of
14
for its treatment
à la
Sommerfeld using complex analysis. At this point, one only needs to know that
(\ell^{\text{loc}},g^{\text{loc}},I_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}},I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}})
are a set of canonical variables for the Hamiltonian
H^{\text{loc}}+H^{\text{log}}
, but are not angle-angle variables anymore (the local action variables undergo small 4PN oscillation under the flow generated by
H^{\text{loc}}+H^{\text{log}}
). I will not yet construct the canonical transformation that goes from
(\ell^{\text{loc}},g^{\text{loc}},I_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}},I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}})
to some action-angle variables for
H^{\text{loc}}+H^{\text{log}}
; instead, I will perform this transformation at the end in Sec.
IV.3
, to obtain an action-angle formulation for the full dynamics
H^{\text{loc}}+H^{\text{log}}+H^{\text{hered}}
.

IV.2
Localizing the hereditary Hamiltonian

The idea is now to perturb
H^{\text{loc}}+H^{\text{log}}
with the nonlocal-in-time, perturbation Hamiltonian
H^{\text{hered}}
. As discussed at the end of Sec.
II.2
, it is licit to ‘naively order-reduce’ the Hamiltonian and compute the on-shell value of the tail piece of the 4PN Hamiltonian, but the resulting motion will be described in terms of new phase-space variables. For my purposes, the map between old and new variables will not be needed. Nonetheless, for completeness, I have worked out this map explicitly in App.
A
. In order to proceed with the ‘naive order-reduction’, I will make use of the explicit map between
(r,\phi,p_{r},p_{\phi})
and
(\ell,g,I_{r\phi},I_{\phi})
at Newtonian order: the Keplerian parametrization. This is in fact the truncation at Newtonian order of the 4PN (local) quasi-Keplerian parametrization of Ref.
39
; the latter is the contact transformation which was implicitly used in Sec.
III
. I also introduce the eccentric anomaly
u(\ell,I_{r\phi},I_{\phi})
, which is defined implicitly by Kepler’s equation
35
:

\displaystyle\ell

\displaystyle=u-e\sin(u)\,.

(55)

The Keplerian parametrization reads

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=a(1-e\cos(u))\,,

(56a)

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle=g+u+2\arctan\Bigl[\frac{\beta(e)\sin u}{1-\beta(e)\cos u}\Bigr]\,,

(56b)

\displaystyle p_{r}

\displaystyle=m\nu\ \sqrt{\frac{Gm}{a}}\ \frac{e\sin(u)}{1-e\cos(u)}\,,

(56c)

\displaystyle p_{\phi}

\displaystyle=I_{\phi}\,,

(56d)

where it should be recalled that
(a,e)
are given by in terms of the action variables by (
52
) and where
\beta(e)=e/(1+\sqrt{1-e^{2}})
. The equations of motion are then given at Newtonian order by
\mathrm{d}\ell/\mathrm{d}t=n
, where the Newtonian expression for the radial frequency reads

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{Gm}{a^{3}}}=\frac{G^{2}m^{5}\nu^{3}}{I_{r\phi}^{3}}\,,

(57)

and by
\mathrm{d}g/\mathrm{d}t=0
. Thus,
g
is a constant, and the reference frame can always be rotated such that
g=0
. Taking a time derivative of the Kepler equations, one also finds that
\mathrm{d}u/\mathrm{d}t=n/(1-e\cos u)
.

The tail piece of the Hamiltonian is expressed in terms of the quadrupole moment, which reads at Newtonian order

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}_{ij}=m\nu r^{2}\left(n^{i}n^{j}-\frac{1}{3}\delta^{ij}\right)=m\nu r^{2}\begin{pmatrix}\cos^{2}\phi-\frac{1}{3}&&\cos\phi\sin\phi&&0\\
\cos\phi\sin\phi&&\sin^{2}\phi-\frac{1}{3}&&0\\
0&&0&&-\frac{1}{3}\end{pmatrix}_{ij}\ .

(58)

Using the Keplerian parametrization (
56
), all components of the quadrupole moment can now be implicitly expressed in terms of the Delaunay variables
(\ell,g,I_{r\phi},I_{\phi})
, through the eccentric anomaly
u(\ell,I_{r\phi},I_{\phi})
. Using the equations of motion, the
N
-th time derivatives
\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(N)}
are found to be expressible (after order reduction) as functions of the Delaunay variables. I finally obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\frac{G^{3}m^{5}\nu^{2}}{3a^{5}}\biggl[\frac{88(1-e^{2})}{(1-e\cos u)^{6}}+\frac{16}{(1-e\cos u)^{5}}-\frac{8}{(1-e\cos u)^{4}}\biggr]\,,

(59)

such that the logarithmic piece of the Hamiltonian, after replacing
\eta
by its expression (
54
), reads in Delaunay variables

\displaystyle H^{\mathrm{log}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2G^{2}m}{5c^{8}}\,\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}\biggl[\ln(1-e \cos u)+\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{Gm}{c^{2}a}\right)+2\ln 2+\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}\biggr]\,.

(60)

Note that I have not localized anything for the logarithmic piece — this is simply the result of the transformation from physical phase-space variable to action angles. Now, in order to localize the hereditary integral explicitly, some more tools are required. Namely, the period nature of a Keplerian orbit needs to be invoked (
via
the Delaunay variables) and its Fourier decomposition is introduced. Hence, the components of the quadrupole moment are decomposed into a Fourier series as

\mathrm{I}_{ij}=\sum_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}{\,}{}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\ell}=\mathcal{I}_{2}\sum_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}{\,}{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\ell},

(61)

where I have used
g=0
and introduced an overall normalization factor

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2}=m\nu a^{2}=\frac{I_{r\phi}^{4}}{G^{2}m^{5}\nu^{3}}\,,

(62)

such that
{\,}{}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}=\mathcal{I}_{2}{\,}{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}
. The various Fourier coefficients are obtained
via
the formula

\displaystyle{}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{d}\ell\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\ell}\,\mathrm{I}_{ij}(\ell,I_{r\phi},I_{\phi})

(63)

and their (normalized) expressions (for
p\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
) are given by Ref.
79

\displaystyle{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{xx}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{3}\frac{3-e^{2}}{e^{2}}\frac{J_{p}(pe)}{p^{2}}+\frac{2(1-e^{2})}{e}\frac{J_{p}^{\prime}(pe)}{p}\,,

(64a)

\displaystyle{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{xy}

\displaystyle=2\mathrm{i}\sqrt{1-e^{2}}\biggl[-\frac{1-e^{2}}{e^{2}}\frac{J_{p}(pe)}{p}+\frac{1}{e}\frac{J_{p}^{\prime}(pe)}{p^{2}}\biggr]\,,

(64b)

\displaystyle{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{yy}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3}\frac{3-2e^{2}}{e^{2}}\frac{J_{p}(pe)}{p^{2}}-\frac{2(1-e^{2})}{e}\frac{J_{p}^{\prime}(pe)}{p}\,,

(64c)

\displaystyle{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{zz}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3}\frac{J_{p}(pe)}{p^{2}}\,,

(64d)

where
J_{p}(x)
are the Bessel functions of the first kind and
J^{\prime}_{p}(x)
are their derivatives (see
8
;
14
;
28
for alternative forms). The
(x,z)
and
(y,z)
components are vanishing, as well as all the
p=0
coefficients. The other components are obtained using the symmetry in the indices, e.g.
{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{yx}={}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{xy}
. Finally, once can check the trace-free character of the quadrupole moment:
{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{xx}+{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{yy}+{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{zz}=0
.
To compute time derivatives, one can use
\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}t=\dot{\ell}(\partial/\partial\ell)+\dot{g}(\partial/\partial g)
, which reduces to
\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}t=n(\partial/\partial\ell)
at this order. The
N
-th derivative of the (order-reduced) quadrupole moment then reads

\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(N)}=\mathcal{I}_{2}\sum_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}(\mathrm{i}pn)^{N}{\,}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\ell}\,,

(65)

where the equations of motion
\mathrm{d}\ell/\mathrm{d}t=n
were used, in accordance with the discussion at the beginning of Sec.
II.2
.
The hereditary tail Hamiltonian (
12
) is now naturally expressible in terms of the Delaunay variables at time
t
and also at time
t^{\prime}=t-\tau
. Note that I have
not
localized anything at this point; I have only performed a contact transformation and a local order reduction. The great advantage of working with Delaunay (action-angle) variables is that their time-evolution (under the Newtonian Hamiltonian) is extremely simple:
I_{r\phi}(t-\tau)=I_{r\phi}(t)
,
I_{\phi}(t-\tau)=I_{\phi}(t)
,
\ell(t-\tau)=\ell(t)-n\tau
,
g(t-\tau)=g(t)-(\omega-n)\tau=0
. However, one must recall that Hamilton’s equations (
II.2
) are integro-differential, such that one is
a priori
not allowed to express the Delaunay variables at time
t^{\prime}
in terms of the Delaunay variables at time
t
using the equations of motion. Nonetheless, as discussed in Sec.
II.2
, it is in fact licit to perform the latter replacements inside the integral in the Hamiltonian if one simultaneously performs at small 4PN contact transformation on the phase space-variables. This was first shown on a toy example in Ref.
48
, but the general expression of the shift was worked out for any pseudo-Hamiltonian in (47) of
33
(see also (19) of
32
), and I compute it explicitly in this case in Appendix
A
. I find that the new set of variables
(\ell^{\prime},g^{\prime},I_{r\phi}^{\prime},I_{\phi}^{\prime})
are related to their local counterparts by

\displaystyle\ell^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\ell^{\text{loc}}-\frac{2\mathrm{i}G^{2}m}{5c^{8}}\sum_{p+q\neq 0}\frac{p^{3}q^{3}}{p+q}n^{5}\Biggl\{\frac{\partial({}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{ij})}{\partial I_{r\phi}}\ln\left|\frac{p}{q}\right|+\frac{1}{n}\frac{\partial n}{\partial I_{r\phi}}\Bigg[1+\left(2+\frac{q}{p+q}\right)\ln\left|\frac{p}{q}\right|\Bigg]{}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}\Biggr\}\ {}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p+q)\ell}\,,

(66a)

\displaystyle g^{\prime}

\displaystyle=g^{\text{loc}}-\frac{2\mathrm{i}G^{2}m}{5c^{8}}\sum_{p+q\neq 0}\frac{p^{3}q^{3}}{p+q}n^{5}\ln\left|\frac{p}{q}\right|\frac{\partial({}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{ij})}{\partial I_{\phi}}{\ }_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}\ \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p+q)\ell}\,,

(66b)

\displaystyle I_{r\phi}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=I_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}}-\frac{2G^{2}m}{5c^{8}}\sum_{p+q\neq 0}\frac{p^{4}q^{3}}{p+q}\ln\left|\frac{p}{q}\right|n^{5}{\ }_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}{\ }{}_{q}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}\ \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p+q)\ell}\,,

(66c)

\displaystyle I_{\phi}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}}\,,

(66d)

where one should recall that
n
and
{}_{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}=\mathcal{I}_{2}\ {}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}
are expressed in terms of the action variables
I_{r\phi}
and
I_{\phi}
; see Eqs. (
57
), (
62
) and (
64
).

As announced, the resulting localized Hamiltonian is obtained by naively replacing the equations of motion inside the hereditary Hamiltonian
6
6

6

In (62) of
33
, (21) of
32
or (247) of
77
, there is a factor
1/2
which is canceled by the factor
2
of (
II.2
); see also Footnote
2
.
, with the additional information that the expression for
H^{\text{loc}}
provided in Eq. (
III.2
) should now be interpreted as a function of
(I_{r\phi}^{\prime},I_{\phi}^{\prime})
rather than a function of
(I_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}},I_{\phi}^{\text{loc}})
.
The Hamiltonian then depends only of the phase-space variables at time
t
(not at
t^{\prime}<t
) and on integrals over a dummy (time-like) variable
\tau
. The latter are computed using the usual formula
61
;
30
;
6
;
24

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\tau\ln\left(\frac{c\tau}{2b_{0}}\right)\mathrm{e}^{inp\tau}=-\frac{1}{pn}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\mathrm{sign}(p)+\mathrm{i}\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}+\mathrm{i}\ln\left(\frac{2|p|nb_{0}}{c}\right)\right)\,.

(67)

Thus, I finally find that the genuinely hereditary piece is given, after localization, by

\displaystyle H^{\mathrm{hered}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2G^{2}m}{5c^{8}}n^{6}\left(\mathcal{I}_{2}\right)^{2}\sum_{(p,q)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}p^{3}q^{3}\ln\left(\frac{|p|}{2}\right){\ }_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}{\ }{}_{q}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p+q)\ell}\,.

(68)

IV.3
Delaunay averaging of the tail Hamiltonian

The new Hamiltonian is now localized, but depends not only on
(I_{r\phi}^{\prime},I_{\phi}^{\prime})
but also on
(\ell^{\prime},g^{\prime})
. Thus,
(I_{r\phi}^{\prime},I_{\phi}^{\prime})
are not constant anymore, and
(\ell^{\prime},g^{\prime},I_{r\phi}^{\prime},I_{\phi}^{\prime})
are not a set of action-angle variables for the perturbed Hamiltonian. To restore the action-angle structure of the Hamiltonian, one performs a so-called Delaunay averaging
51
;
52
, which was first applied to this problem by Refs.
47
;
48
following the methods laid out in Ref.
38
. This consists in performing a
