canonical
transformation on the phase-space variables whose effect is to remove the dependence on the angle variables in the Hamiltonian. Since this dependence manifests itself only in the oscillatory pieces of the Fourier decomposition, one is then left only with the non-oscillatory piece after the procedure. Thus, in practice, the new ‘Delaunay averaged’ Hamiltonian will be obtained
via
naive orbit-averaging. Here, I will follow the treatment laid out in Sec. 12.4 of Ref.
60
, and only quote the results in that textbook at leading order in the perturbations (note that my conventions differ by factors of
2\pi
); the reader is invited to refer to it for the detailed derivation.

From (12.65) of
60
, one learns that the generating function
\mathcal{Y}(\ell^{\prime},g^{\prime},I_{r\phi},I_{\phi})
for the canonical transformation reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}(\ell^{\prime},g^{\prime},I_{r\phi},I_{\phi})=\ell^{\prime}I_{r\phi}+g^{\prime}I_{\phi}+\delta\mathcal{Y}(\ell^{\prime},g^{\prime},I_{r\phi},I_{\phi})+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{c^{10}}\right)\,,

(69)

where
\delta\mathcal{Y}
must satisfy, according to Eq. (12.76) of Ref.
60
,

\displaystyle n^{\prime}\frac{\partial(\delta\mathcal{Y})}{\partial\ell^{\prime}}+(\omega^{\prime}-n^{\prime})\frac{\partial(\delta\mathcal{Y})}{\partial g^{\prime}}=\langle H^{\text{tail}}\rangle-H^{\text{tail}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{c^{10}}\right)\,.

(70)

Here, I have introduced the average of
f
as
\langle f\rangle=(2\pi)^{-2}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{d}\ell\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{d}g\ f(\ell,g,I_{r\phi},I_{\phi})
.
Neglecting 5PN terms (i.e., 1PN terms relatively to the 4PN Hamiltonian) and using
\omega-n=\mathcal{O}(1/c^{2})
, this simplifies to

\displaystyle n\frac{\partial(\delta\mathcal{Y})}{\partial\ell}=-\widetilde{H}^{\text{tail}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{c^{10}}\right)\,,

(71)

where the oscillatory piece is denoted with a tilde, namely

\widetilde{f}=f-\langle f\rangle
.
Moreover, one requires that
\langle\delta\mathcal{Y}\rangle=0
. One is also free to choose
\delta\mathcal{Y}
such that is does not depend on
g^{\prime}
at this order.
The Hamiltonian in the new (genuinely action-angle) variables will then only depend on
(I_{r\phi},I_{\phi})
, and will be obtained ‘practically’ by removing
(\ell^{\prime},g^{\prime})
from the Hamiltonian in terms of
(\ell^{\prime},g^{\prime},I_{r\phi}^{\prime},I_{\phi}^{\prime})
by orbit-averaging, and then performing the ‘naive’ replacements
I_{r\phi}^{\prime}\rightarrow I_{r\phi}
and
I_{\phi}^{\prime}\rightarrow I_{\phi}
.

I will first obtain the expression of the orbit-averaged Hamiltonian; I will solve for
\delta\mathcal{Y}
in (
71
) in a second step.
The logarithmic Hamiltonian
H^{\mathrm{log}}
can be exactly orbit-averaged without the need for an expansion in Fourier series. Recall that the Newtonian problem is degenerate, such that the orbit average of a function of eccentric anomaly
f(u)
reads

\displaystyle\left\langle f\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{d}\ell\,f(u(\ell))=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{d}u\,(1-e\cos u)f(u)\,,

(72)

where I have used
\mathrm{d}\ell/\mathrm{d}u=1-e\cos(u)
at this order; see (
55
).
The orbit averaging procedure is then performed using the formulas provided in (8.4) and (8.6) of
7
, which I reproduce here:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\mathrm{d}u}{(1-e\cos u)^{N}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{N-1}}{(N-1)!}\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{N-1}}{\mathrm{d}y^{N-1}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{y^{2}-e^{2}}}\right)\right]_{y=1}\,,

(73a)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{d}u\ \frac{\ln(1-e\cos u)}{(1-e\cos u)^{N}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{N-1}}{(N-1)!}\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}^{N-1}Y(y;e)}{\mathrm{d}y^{N-1}}\right]_{y=1}\,,

(73b)

where I have introduced
7

\displaystyle Y(y;e)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{y^{2}-e^{2}}}\Biggl\{\ln\left[\frac{\sqrt{1-e^{2}}+1}{2}\right]+2\ln\left[1+\frac{\sqrt{1-e^{2}}-1}{y+\sqrt{y^{2}-e^{2}}}\right]\Biggr\}\,.

(74)

Thus, one finds that

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{G^{3}m^{5}\nu^{2}(92+292e^{2}+37e^{4})}{3a^{5}(1-e^{2})^{7/2}}\,,

(75a)

\displaystyle\left\langle\ln\Bigl(1-e\cos(u)\Bigr)\,\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}\mathrm{I}_{ij}^{(3)}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{G^{3}m^{5}\nu^{2}}{36a^{5}(1-e^{2})^{7/2}}\Biggl[-2408-3792e^{2}-255e^{4}+(2408+2692e^{2})\sqrt{1-e^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+(1152+3504e^{2}+444e^{4})\ln\left(\frac{2(1-e^{2})}{1+\sqrt{1-e^{2}}}\right)\Biggr]\,.

(75b)

Thanks to these expressions, I finally obtain

\displaystyle\boxed{\begin{aligned} \left\langle H^{\mathrm{log}}\right\rangle&=\frac{G^{5}m^{6}\nu^{2}}{a^{5}c^{8}(1-e^{2})^{7/2}}\Biggl\{-\frac{1204}{45}-\frac{632}{15}e^{2}-\frac{17}{6}e^{4}+\frac{1204+1346e^{2}}{45}\sqrt{1-e^{2}}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+\frac{96+292e^{2}+37e^{4}}{15}\Biggl[2\ln\left(\frac{1-e^{2}}{1+\sqrt{1-e^{2}}}\right)+\ln\left(\frac{Gm}{c^{2}a}\right)+6\ln 2+2\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}\Biggr]\Biggr\}\,.\end{aligned}}

(75)

The only task left to complete is the orbit-averaging of the
localized
tail Hamiltonian (
68
). Since it is already expressed as a Fourier series, the orbit-averaging is straightforward: only terms satisfying
p+q=0
survive the orbit averaging, and one immediately finds that

\displaystyle\left\langle H^{\mathrm{hered}}\right\rangle=\frac{2G^{2}m}{5c^{8}}n^{6}\left(\mathcal{I}_{2}\right)^{2}\sum_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}p^{6}\ln\left(\frac{|p|}{2}\right){\ }_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}{\ }{}_{-p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}\,.

(76)

As in Eq. (4.1) of Ref.
84
, I define the
enhancement function

\displaystyle\boxed{\Lambda_{0}(e)=\frac{1}{16}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{6}\ln\left(\frac{p}{2}\right){\ }_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}{\ }{}_{-p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}\,,}

(77)

where one should recall that the coefficients
{\ }{}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}
are expressed only in terms of
e
. Replacing the normalization factor and the radial frequency, one then simply finds that

\displaystyle\boxed{\left\langle H^{\mathrm{hered}}\right\rangle=\frac{64G^{5}m^{6}\nu^{2}}{5a^{5}c^{8}}\Lambda_{0}(e)\,.}

(78)

I have compared the small-eccentricity expansion of
\langle H^{\mathrm{tail}}\rangle=\langle H^{\mathrm{log}}\rangle+\langle H^{\mathrm{hered}}\rangle
with Eqs. (52) and (53) of Ref.
39
. Keeping in mind that the fractions appearing in that work are not genuine fractions but approximations for floating-point numbers
1
, I have found
numerical
agreement with that work up to
\mathcal{O}(e^{12})
terms which are uncontrolled in their approach.

Finally, the oscillatory part of the hereditary Hamiltonian is trivially given by the criteria
p+q\neq 0
, namely

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}^{\mathrm{hered}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2G^{2}m}{5c^{8}}n^{6}\left(\mathcal{I}_{2}\right)^{2}\sum_{p+q\neq 0}p^{3}q^{3}\ln\left(\frac{|p|}{2}\right){\ }_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}{\ }{}_{q}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p+q)\ell}\,.

(79)

In order to express explicitly the oscillatory part of the logarithmic piece of the Hamiltonian, one needs to perform the expansion of
\ln(1-e\cos u)
as a Fourier series, which reads

\displaystyle\ln(1-e\cos u)=\sum_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}{}_{p}\widehat{L}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\ell}\,,

(80)

where Eq. (
63
) leads to the explicit expression

\displaystyle{}_{p}\widehat{L}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{d}u\,(1-e\cos u)\,\ln(1-e \cos u)\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}np(u-e\sin u)}\,.

(81)

These integrals are
a priori
not expressible in closed form but can be either expanded for small eccentricity or related to the class of extended Bessel functions introduced in Eq. (23) of Ref.
78
. The oscillatory piece then reads

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}^{\text{log}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2G^{2}m}{5c^{8}}n^{6}(\mathcal{I}_{2})^{2}\Biggl\{\sum_{p+q+r\neq 0}\!\!\!\!\!p^{3}q^{3}{\,}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}{\,}{}_{q}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}{\,}{}_{r}{\widehat{L}}\ \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p+q+r)\ell}+\left(\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{Gm}{c^{2}a}\right)+2\ln 2+\gamma_{E}\right)\!\!\sum_{p+q\neq 0}\!\!\!p^{3}q^{3}{\,}_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}{\,}{}_{q}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}\ \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p+q)\ell}\Biggr\}\,.

(82)

This provides an explicit expression for the oscillatory tail Hamiltonian
\widetilde{H}^{\text{tail}}=\widetilde{H}^{\text{log}}+\widetilde{H}^{\text{hered}}
, where the individual pieces were computed in Eqs. (
82
) and (
79
). This allows for the explicit integration of Eq. (
71
) with respect to
\ell
; recalling that
\langle\delta\mathcal{Y}\rangle=0
and that
\delta\mathcal{Y}
should not depend of
g^{\prime}
, I find

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{Y}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\mathrm{i}G^{2}m}{5c^{8}}n^{4}(\mathcal{I}_{2})^{2}\Biggl\{\sum_{p+q+r\neq 0}\frac{p^{3}q^{3}}{p+q+r}{\ }_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}{\ }{}_{q}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}{\ }{}_{r}{\widehat{L}}\ \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p+q+r)\ell^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\left(\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{Gm}{c^{2}a}\right)+2\ln 2+\gamma_{E}+\ln\left(\frac{|p|}{2}\right)\right)\sum_{p+q\neq 0}\frac{p^{3}q^{3}}{p+q}{\ }_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}{\ }{}_{q}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}\ \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(p+q)\ell^{\prime}}\Biggr\}\,.

(83)

Finally, the canonical transformation associated with the Delaunay averaging is given, according to Eq. (12.68) and (12.73) of Ref.
60
, by

\displaystyle\ell

\displaystyle=\ell^{\prime}+\frac{\partial(\delta\mathcal{Y})}{\partial I_{r\phi}^{\prime}}\ ,

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=\ell^{\prime}+\frac{\partial(\delta\mathcal{Y})}{\partial I_{\phi}^{\prime}}\ ,

\displaystyle I_{r\phi}

\displaystyle=I_{r\phi}^{\prime}-\frac{\partial(\delta\mathcal{Y})}{\partial\ell^{\prime}}\ ,

\displaystyle I_{\phi}

\displaystyle=I_{\phi}^{\prime}\ .

(84)

Since at Newtonian order, the Hamiltonian
H=-G^{2}m^{5}\nu^{3}/(2I_{r\phi}^{2})\,+\,\mathcal{O}(1/c^{2})
only depends on
I_{r\phi}
, it is straightforward to verify that the shift
\delta I_{r\phi}
given in Eq. (
IV.3
) leads to a variation of the Hamiltonian
\delta H=n\,\delta I_{r\phi}
which exactly cancels out the oscillatory piece of the Hamiltonian given in Eqs. (
82
) and (
79
); only the secular piece remains. The shifts with respect to the other variables have the only purpose of preserving the canonical nature of the contact transformation, which follows automatically from the existence of a generating function.

IV.4
Resummation of the tail enhancement function

In order to obtain an explicit expression for
\left\langle H^{\mathrm{hered}}\right\rangle
(without an unwieldy infinite sum), one approach is to perform a small eccentricity expansion of
\Lambda_{0}(e)
, defined in (
77
); thanks to the properties of the Bessel functions, any finite truncation in the eccentricity expansion has the immediate effect of truncating the Fourier sum. Thus, I find that the
e\rightarrow 0
expansion of this enhancement function reads

\displaystyle\Lambda_{0}(e)

\displaystyle\underset{e\rightarrow 0}{\sim}e^{2}\left[-\frac{277}{24}\ln 2+\frac{729}{64}\ln 3\right]+e^{4}\left[\frac{11353}{96}\ln 2-\frac{13851}{256}\ln 3\right]

\displaystyle\quad+e^{6}\left[-\frac{21997}{32}\ln 2+\frac{419661}{4096}\ln 3+\frac{9765625}{36864}\ln 5\right]+e^{8}\left[\frac{5056751}{2304}\ln 2+\frac{26915409}{32768}\ln 3-\frac{419921875}{294912}\ln 5\right]

\displaystyle\quad+e^{10}\left[-\frac{4852988101}{691200}\ln 2-\frac{138733913079}{26214400}\ln 3+\frac{93681640625}{28311552}\ln 5+\frac{678223072849}{235929600}\ln 7\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(e^{12}\right);

(85)

see the Supplemental Material
2
for higher orders in
e
.
However, it is well known that the small eccentricity expansion performs very badly even for moderate eccentricities; see Fig.
1
. To solve this problem, two groups
79
;
57
;
84
;
83
have independently investigated the asymptotic properties of similar enhancement functions in the limit where
e\rightarrow 1
, allowing them to resum the enhancement functions in a way that preserves accuracy for any eccentricity. This is possible because
all
the terms entering the infinite sum are controlled analytically. Following the recipe laid out in Sec. 5.1 of
79
, I computed the asymptotic expansion of
\Lambda_{0}(e)
. This relies on the observation that the sum (
77
) is dominated by the
p\rightarrow\infty
terms as
e\rightarrow 1
. Thus, the procedure consists in three steps: (i) compute the uniform asymptotic expansion of each term in the sum as
p\rightarrow\infty
(this involves expanding Bessel functions, whose uniform asymptotic expansions involve Airy functions, or equivalently, modified Bessel functions of the second kind); (ii) replace the sum by an integral and compute it explicitly (the integrands all come as quadratic products of modified Bessel functions of the second kind); and (iii) Taylor expand the result as
\epsilon=1-e^{2}\rightarrow 0
. This procedure in in fact well defined only for the first few terms in the expansion; after a certain
breakdown order
, which usually corresponds to terms of order
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0})
, an infinite number of integrals contribute to each order and the procedure becomes ill-defined
79
. This is not a problem
per se
because the resulting expansion is a (divergent) asymptotic series anyway: for such series, keeping only the few first terms in the expansion usually yields a very good approximation of the original function, but adding more terms can degrade the agreement and eventually lead to a divergence. Thus, there exists an
e
-dependent
optimal truncation
which minimizes the error incurred by the expansion; the resulting optimal expansion is usually called “superasymptotic”
37
. Here, I have only kept the first three terms in the expansion, and have not investigated what the optimal truncation order was (this order would depend on
e
anyway), nor whether the neglected
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{-1/2})
contribution is in fact well defined by this procedure. I find that the asymptotic behavior of the enhancement function as
e\rightarrow 1
reads

\displaystyle\Lambda_{0}(e)\underset{e\rightarrow 1}{\sim}\quad

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1-e^{2})^{7/2}}\Biggl[\frac{65}{3}-\frac{425}{96}\gamma_{\text{E}}-\frac{425}{24}\ln 2-\frac{425}{192}\ln 3-\frac{425}{64}\ln(1-e^2)\Biggr]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1-e^{2})^{5/2}}\Biggl[-\frac{3301}{160}+\frac{61}{16}\gamma_{\text{E}}+\frac{61}{4}\ln 2+\frac{61}{32}\ln 3+\frac{183}{32}\ln\left(1-e^{2}\right)\Biggr]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1-e^{2})^{3/2}}\Biggl[\frac{31707}{11200}-\frac{37}{96}\gamma_{\text{E}}-\frac{37}{24}\ln 2-\frac{37}{192}\ln 3-\frac{37}{64}\ln\left(1-e^{2}\right)\Biggr]+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-e^{2}}}\right)\,.

(86)

Crucially, one notices that factoring out
\ln(1-e^2)
reveals a prefactor that resums exactly (up to a factor
-3/2
) into the Peters and Mathews enhancement function

\displaystyle f(e)=\frac{1}{16}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}\,p^{6}{\ }_{p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}{\ }{}_{-p}\widehat{\mathrm{I}}_{ij}=\frac{1}{(1-e^{2})^{7/2}}\left(1+\frac{73}{24}e^{2}+\frac{37}{96}e^{4}\right)\,;

(87)

notice that this infinite sum would be proportional to Eq. (
77
) if it were not for the
\ln(p/2)
contribution.
Based on the behavior of similar enhancement functions, see, e.g., (171) and (172) of
79
, I will assume that the logarithmic factor is in fact exact, namely that there are no more logarithmic terms at higher orders in the expansion. Thus, I recover the behavior predicted by (1.1), (1.3), (1.5) and (4.3) of Ref.
84
:

\displaystyle\Lambda_{0}(e)\underset{e\rightarrow 1}{\sim}-\frac{3}{2}f(e)\ln(1-e^2)\,.

(88)

This leads to the following proposed resummation:

\displaystyle\boxed{\Lambda_{0}(e)=-\frac{3}{2}\biggl[f(e)\ln(1-e^2)+\frac{e^{2}}{(1-e^{2})^{7/2}}\lambda_{0}(e)\biggr]\,,{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\Bigg(}\!\!\!\!}

(89)

where
\lambda(e)
is
defined
by the relation (
89
). In practice,
\lambda_{0}(e)
will be replaced by its truncated small eccentricity expansion

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}^{[N]}(e)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=0}^{N}\alpha_{p}e^{2p}\,,

(90)

from which one defines the approximate (or truncated) resummation

\displaystyle\Lambda_{0}^{[N]}(e)=-\frac{3}{2}\Biggl[f(e)\ln(1-e^2)+\frac{e^{2}}{(1-e^{2})^{7/2}}\lambda_{0}^{[N]}(e)\Biggr]\,.

(91)

In order to ensure that same small-eccentricity expansions of
\Lambda_{0}(e)
and
\Lambda_{0}^{[N]}(e)
coincide, one requires than the coefficients are given (for
N=4
) by

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}

\displaystyle=1+\frac{277}{36}\ln 2-\frac{243}{32}\ln 3\,,

(92a)

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{85}{24}-\frac{5077}{48}\ln 2+\frac{8019}{128}\ln 3\,,

(92b)

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{215}{96}+\frac{6143}{8}\ln 2-\frac{466479}{2048}\ln 3-\frac{9765625}{55296}\ln 5\,,

(92c)

\displaystyle\alpha_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{839}{576}-\frac{5925115}{1728}\ln 2-\frac{2197287}{16384}\ln 3+\frac{693359375}{442368}\ln 5\,,

(92d)

\displaystyle\alpha_{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{49}{45}+\frac{3105571069}{259200}\ln 2+\frac{66387426093}{13107200}\ln 3-\frac{267587890625}{42467328}\ln 5-\frac{678223072849}{353894400}\ln 7\,;

(92e)

see the Supplemental Material
2
for higher-order coefficients.

Moreover, sparked by a comment in Ref.
84
, which claims that “it is not necessary to isolate the logarithmic divergence in
\Lambda_{2}(e)
” [where
\Lambda_{2}(e)
is another enhancement function in the same family as
\Lambda_{0}(e)
], I also considered the following naive, truncated resummation:

\displaystyle{\Lambda}_{0,\text{naive}}^{[N]}(e)=\frac{e^{2}}{(1-e^{2})^{7/2}}\sum_{p=0}^{N}\alpha^{\text{naive}}_{p}e^{2p}\,,

(93)

where the coefficient
\alpha^{\text{naive}}_{p}
are immediately determined by requiring that the small-eccentricity expansions of
\Lambda_{0}(e)
and
{\Lambda}_{0,\text{naive}}^{[N]}(e)
coincide.

I now compare these resummations with a numerical estimate for
\Lambda_{0}(e)
This numerical estimate was obtained by truncating the sum
\Lambda_{0}(e)
to some order
p_{\text{max}}(e)
, which is determined
ad hoc
by requiring that the relative error between the numerical estimate and the resummation
\Lambda_{0}^{[4]}(e)
has converged to a stable value. Note that for better precision, I did not expand the Bessel functions in small eccentricity when computing this numerical estimate
78
. I obtained this estimate for
e\in\{k/100,k\in[\![1,99]\!]\}
, and the truncation order of Eq. (
77
) increases with eccentricity: for
e=0.2
,
p_{\mathrm{max}}=20
modes are enough, whereas for
e=0.99
,
p_{\mathrm{max}}=15\,000
modes were required. In Fig.
1
, I compare the following quantities, which are normalized by the divergent factor
(1-e^{2})^{-7/2}
: (i) the numerical estimate; (ii) the small eccentricity expansion (
IV.4
) of
\Lambda_{0}(e)
, neglecting terms that are
\mathcal{O}(e^{12})
; (iii) the naive resummation (
93
) with
N=4
; and (iv) my proposed resummation (
91
) with
N=4
. I find that the small eccentricity expansion completely breaks down for
e\gtrsim 0.5
, but that both resummations perform well for large eccentricities. When focusing on eccentricities greater than
0.8
, it is moreover clear that the proposed resummation (
91
) is much closer to the numerical value than the naive resummation (
93
): the relative error on
\Lambda_{0}(e)
for the proposed resummation (with
N=4
) remains smaller than
4\cdot 10^{-6}
for any value of the eccentricity! One concludes that it is very useful to control the logarithmic behavior of the enhancement function, and that the resummed enhancement function (
91
) is both computationally efficient and numerically accurate, even for eccentricities very close to
1
. Note that an analogous comparison was carried out in Fig. 1 of Ref.
63
; there, the errors were dominated by the numerical noise.

e
(1-e^{2})^{7/2}\,\Lambda_{0}(e)
{|\Lambda_{0}-\Lambda_{0}^{[4]}|}/{\left|\Lambda_{0}\right|}

Figure 1:

(Top panel)
Comparison of various estimates for
\Lambda_{0}(e)
, normalized by the divergent factor
(1-e^{2})^{-7/2}
: (i) the black dots are the data points for the reference numerical estimate; (ii) the blue curve corresponds to the small-eccentricity expansion (
IV.4
), including terms up to
\mathcal{O}(e^{10})
; (iii) the red curve corresponds to the naive resummation (
93
) with
N=4
; and (iv) the green curve corresponds to the proposed resummation (
91
) with
N=4
. The small eccentricity expansion becomes fully inadequate for
e\geq 0.5
; whereas both resummations provide a reasonable estimate for larger
e
.
(Inset plot in top panel)

0.8\leq e\leq 1
. The ‘naive’ resummation is not accurate in this regime, whereas the proposed resummation is extremely accurate.

Relative error of the proposed resummation
\Lambda_{0}^{[N]}(e)
for
N=4
[see Eq. (
91
)], with respect to the numerical estimate for
\Lambda_{0}(e)
. For any value of the eccentricity, the relative error always remains smaller than
4\cdot 10^{-6}
. For
e\leq 0.15
, the error is dominated by numerical noise, due to the division by
|\Lambda_{0}(e)|
which vanishes in the
e\rightarrow 0
limit. For
e>0.15
, the error is dominated by the inaccuracy of the resummation; this required controlling the numerical estimate very precisely by summing over many modes. For
e=0.2
,
p_{\mathrm{max}}=20
modes are enough, whereas for
e=0.99
,
p_{\mathrm{max}}=15\,000
modes were required!

IV.5
Tail Hamiltonian in terms of action variables

Using the Newtonian relation between the Keplerian parameters and the action variables (
52
), I find that the tail Hamiltonian is expressed in terms of the action variables as follows:

\displaystyle H^{\text{log}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m\nu^{2}}{c^{8}}\Biggl\{\frac{1}{i_{\phi}^{\,7}i_{r\phi}^{\,3}}\left(-\frac{1291}{18}+\frac{170}{3}\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}\right)+\frac{170}{3i_{\phi}^{\,6}i_{r\phi}^{\,4}}+\frac{1}{i_{\phi}^{\,5}i_{r\phi}^{\,5}}\left(\frac{239}{5}-\frac{244}{5}\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}\right)-\frac{1346}{45i_{\phi}^{\,4}i_{r\phi}^{\,6}}+\frac{1}{i_{\phi}^{\,3}i_{r\phi}^{\,7}}\left(-\frac{17}{6}+\frac{74}{15}\gamma_{\mathrm{E}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\ln\left(\frac{c\,i_{r\phi}^{\,2}(i_{\phi}+i_{r\phi})}{8i_{\phi}^{\,2}}\right)\left[-\frac{170}{3i_{\phi}^{\,7}i_{r\phi}^{\,3}}+\frac{244}{5i_{\phi}^{\,5}i_{r\phi}^{\,5}}-\frac{74}{15i_{\phi}^{\,3}i_{r\phi}^{\,7}}\right]\Biggr\}\,,

(94a)

\displaystyle H^{\text{hered}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m\nu^{2}}{c^{8}}\Biggl\{\ln\left(\frac{i_{r\phi}}{i_{\phi}}\right)\left[\frac{170}{\,i_{\phi}^{\,7}i_{r\phi}^{\,3}}-\frac{732}{5\,i_{\phi}^{\,5}i_{r\phi}^{\,5}}+\frac{74}{5\,i_{\phi}^{\,3}i_{r\phi}^{\,7}}\right]+\frac{96}{5}\lambda_{0}\!\left(\sqrt{1-\frac{i_{\phi}^{\,2}}{i_{r\phi}^{\,2}}}\right)\left[\frac{1}{i_{\phi}^{\,5}i_{r\phi}^{\,5}}-\frac{1}{i_{\phi}^{\,7}i_{r\phi}^{\,3}}\right]\Biggr\}\,,

(94b)

where
\lambda_{0}
is defined by (
77
) and (
89
) but is well approximated by (
90
) and (
92
).

The full Hamiltonian has now been obtained in terms of action variables

\displaystyle H(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})=H^{\mathrm{loc}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})+H^{\mathrm{log}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})+H^{\mathrm{hered}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})\,,

(95)

where
H^{\mathrm{loc}}(i_{\phi},i_{r\phi})
was given by Eq. (
III.2
), but which is now to be understood as a relation in terms of the new, perturbed action variables
(i_{\phi},i_{r\phi})
[rather than in terms of the old, nonperturbed, local action variables
(i_{r\phi}^{\mathrm{loc}},i_{\phi}^{\mathrm{loc}})
].

In the rest of this work, I will analogously split various quantities into a local part, a logarithmic part and a hereditary part. Let me now define precisely what is meant by this split. Consider a quantity
Q[\mathcal{H}]
which is a functional of some Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
. The local piece of
Q
is denoted by
Q^{\text{loc}}=Q[H^{\text{loc}}]
, and is defined as the quantity obtained if we only consider the local piece of the Hamiltonian. The logarithmic piece of
Q
is then denoted by
Q^{\text{log}}=Q[H^{\text{loc}}+H^{\text{log}}]-Q^{\text{loc}}
, which is defined as the quantity one needs to add to
Q^{\text{loc}}
if one now considers both the local and logarithmic pieces, ignores the hereditary piece. Finally, one defines the hereditary piece
Q^{\text{hered}}=Q[H^{\text{loc}}+H^{\text{log}}+H^{\text{hered}}]-Q^{\text{loc}}-Q^{\text{log}}
, namely the missing piece one needs to add to
Q^{\text{loc}}+Q^{\text{log}}
in order to account for the full physical Hamiltonian. Thus, of course, the only meaningful physical quantity is
Q=Q^{\text{loc}}+Q^{\text{log}}+Q^{\text{hered}}
, but this split is handy for presentation and computational purposes. One subtlety is that the quantity
Q
can sometimes be expressed in terms of two different pairs of variables, say
(x_{1},y_{1})
and
(x_{2},y_{2})
, which leads to the splits
Q(x_{1},y_{1})=Q^{\text{loc}}(x_{1},y_{1})+Q^{\text{log}}(x_{1},y_{1})+Q^{\text{hered}}(x_{1},y_{1})
and
Q(x_{2},y_{2})=Q^{\text{loc}}(x_{2},y_{2})+Q^{\text{log}}(x_{2},y_{2})+Q^{\text{hered}}(x_{2},y_{2})
. Although the numerical equality
Q(x_{1},y_{1})=Q(x_{2},y_{2})
always holds (up to neglected higher-order PN terms), this is not always the case for the individual pieces; e.g., one can have
Q^{\text{hered}}(x_{1},y_{1})\neq Q^{\text{hered}}(x_{2},y_{2})
. This is because there can be indirect logarithmic and hereditary corrections arising from the choice of variables.

IV.6
Tail contributions to the radial action in terms of energy and angular momentum

It is now straightforward to invert the map between energy and radial action.
The local contribution to
i_{r\phi}
in terms of
(\varepsilon,j)
has already been given in Eq. (
44
).
Then, recalling that
E=H(i_{\phi},i_{r\phi})
and
J=I_{\phi}
, I computed using Eq. (
94
) the correction needed to account for the logarithmic piece in the Hamiltonian, which reads

\displaystyle i_{r\phi}^{\text{log}}=i_{r}^{\text{log}}=\frac{\nu\,\varepsilon^{7/2}}{c\,j^{3/2}}\Biggl\{\frac{17}{6}+\frac{1346}{45\sqrt{j}}-\frac{239}{5j}-\frac{170}{3j^{3/2}}+\frac{1291}{18j^{2}}+\left(\frac{74}{15}-\frac{244}{5j}+\frac{170}{3j^{2}}\right)\left[\ln\left(\frac{1+\sqrt{j}}{8j\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right)-\gamma_{\text{E}}\right]\Biggr\}\,.

(96a)

Finally, the last correction, needed to account for the hereditary term in the Hamiltonian, follows from Eq. (
94b
) and reads

\displaystyle i_{r\phi}^{\text{hered}}

\displaystyle=i_{r}^{\text{hered}}=\frac{\varepsilon^{7/2}\nu}{cj^{3/2}}\Biggl\{\ln j\left(\frac{37}{5}-\frac{366}{5j}+\frac{85}{j^{2}}\right)+\frac{96}{5}\lambda_{0}\!\left(\sqrt{1-j}\right)\left(\frac{1}{j^{2}}-\frac{1}{j}\right)\Biggr\}\,.

(96b)

The final inverse maps are thus

\displaystyle i_{r\phi}(\varepsilon,j)

\displaystyle=i_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}}(\varepsilon,j)+i_{r\phi}^{\text{log}}(\varepsilon,j)+i_{r\phi}^{\text{hered}}(\varepsilon,j)\,,

(97a)

\displaystyle i_{r}(\varepsilon,j)

\displaystyle=i_{r}^{\text{loc}}(\varepsilon,j)+i_{r}^{\text{log}}(\varepsilon,j)+i_{r}^{\text{hered}}(\varepsilon,j)\,,

(97b)

where the various components are given explicitly by Eqs. (
44
) and (
94
).

V
Fundamental frequencies in terms of energy and angular momentum

V.1
Radial and azimuthal frequencies
(n,\omega)

The fundamental frequencies are straightforward to obtain in the action-angle approach, since they are by construction conjugate to action variables; see Secs.
III.3
and
IV
. Since the full Hamiltonian
H=H^{\text{loc}}+H^{\text{log}}+H^{\text{hered}}
has now been constructed in terms of action variables, one can immediately obtain the radial and azimuthal frequencies through partial differentiation
60
. Here, it will be instructive to follow the conventions of Sec.
IV.5
to split these frequencies into local, logarithmic and hereditary terms, which are defined as

\displaystyle n^{\text{loc}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Gm^{2}\nu}\frac{\partial H^{\text{loc}}}{\partial i_{r\phi}}\Bigg|_{i_{\phi}}\,,

\displaystyle\omega^{\text{loc}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})

\displaystyle=n^{\text{loc}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})+\frac{1}{Gm^{2}\nu}\frac{\partial H^{\text{loc}}}{\partial i_{\phi}}\Bigg|_{i_{r\phi}}\,,

(98a)

\displaystyle n^{\text{log}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Gm^{2}\nu}\frac{\partial H^{\text{log}}}{\partial i_{r\phi}}\Bigg|_{i_{\phi}}\,,

\displaystyle\omega^{\text{log}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})

\displaystyle=n^{\text{log}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})+\frac{1}{Gm^{2}\nu}\frac{\partial H^{\text{log}}}{\partial i_{\phi}}\Bigg|_{i_{r\phi}}\,,

(98b)

\displaystyle n^{\text{hered}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Gm^{2}\nu}\frac{\partial H^{\text{hered}}}{\partial i_{r\phi}}\Bigg|_{i_{\phi}}\,,

\displaystyle\omega^{\text{hered}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})

\displaystyle=n^{\text{hered}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})+\frac{1}{Gm^{2}\nu}\frac{\partial H^{\text{hered}}}{\partial i_{\phi}}\Bigg|_{i_{r\phi}}\,.

(98c)

The total frequencies in terms of action variables read

\displaystyle n(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})

\displaystyle=n^{\text{loc}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})+n^{\text{log}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})+n^{\text{hered}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})\,,

(99a)

\displaystyle\omega(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})

\displaystyle=\omega^{\text{loc}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})+\omega^{\text{log}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})+\omega^{\text{hered}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})\,,

(99b)

where different contributions to
n
and
\omega
are given, respectively, in Eqs. (
148
) and (
149
).
It is then immediate to replace the action variables
(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})
by their full expressions Eq. (
97
) in terms of energy and angular momentum
(\varepsilon,j)
, including both local and tail contributions. The map between fundamental frequencies
(n,\omega)
and energy and angular momentum
(\varepsilon,j)
is obtained schematically by

\displaystyle n(\varepsilon,j)

\displaystyle=\Biggl[n^{\text{loc}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})+n^{\text{log}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})+n^{\text{hered}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})\Biggr]_{i_{r\phi}\,\rightarrow\,i_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}}(\varepsilon,j)\,+\,i_{r\phi}^{\text{log}}\,,(\varepsilon,j)\,+\,i_{r\phi}^{\text{hered}}(\varepsilon,j)}\quad,

(100a)

\displaystyle\omega(\varepsilon,j)

\displaystyle=\Biggl[\omega^{\text{loc}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})+\omega^{\text{log}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})\,+\,\omega^{\text{hered}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})\Biggr]_{i_{r\phi}\,\rightarrow\,i_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}}(\varepsilon,j)\,+\,i_{r\phi}^{\text{log}}(\varepsilon,j)\,+\,i_{r\phi}^{\text{hered}}(\varepsilon,j)}\quad,

(100b)

where
i_{\phi}
is trivially replaced in terms of
(\varepsilon,j)
using Eq. (
43
).
From Eq. (
100
), it can be seen that the tail part appearing in Eq. (
101
) in fact arises in two distinct ways: there is (i) a direct contribution, arising from the logarithmic and hereditary pieces of the fundamental frequencies in terms of action variables provided in Eq. (
99
); and (ii) an indirect contribution, arising from the replacement of the action variable
i_{r\phi}
appearing in the local, Newtonian piece of Eqs. (
99
) by its 4PN expression (
96
) in terms of
(\varepsilon,j)
, which contains tail contributions.
The direct contribution is in (numerical) agreement with Eqs. (54)–(57) of Ref.
39
, but the indirect contribution was overlooked in that work; the full map between
(n,\omega)
and
(\varepsilon,j)
presented hereafter is thus novel.

The fundamental frequencies in terms of energy and angular momentum are then split as usual into

\displaystyle n(\varepsilon,j)

\displaystyle=n^{\text{loc}}(\varepsilon,j)+n^{\text{log}}(\varepsilon,j)+n^{\text{hered}}(\varepsilon,j)\,,

(101a)

\displaystyle\omega(\varepsilon,j)

\displaystyle=\omega^{\text{loc}}(\varepsilon,j)+\omega^{\text{log}}(\varepsilon,j)+\omega^{\text{hered}}(\varepsilon,j)\,,

(101b)

where the different contributions to
n
and
\omega
are given, respectively, in Eqs. (
150
) and (
151
). From the previous discussion, it is known that this split differs from that of Eq. (
99
), such that
n^{\text{log}}(\varepsilon,j)\neq n^{\text{log}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})
and
n^{\text{hered}}(\varepsilon,j)\neq n^{\text{hered}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})
. The conventions that were chosen for this split were described in general in Sec.
IV.5
, but for clarity, I will remind the reader what the definitions are in the particular case of
n(\varepsilon,j)
. The local piece is defined ignoring everything else, namely

\displaystyle n^{\text{loc}}(\varepsilon,j)=\big[n^{\text{loc}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})\big]_{i_{r\phi}\rightarrow i_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}}(\varepsilon,j)}\,.

(102a)

The logarithmic piece is then added as a perturbation to the local piece, namely

\displaystyle n^{\text{log}}(\varepsilon,j)=\big[n^{\text{loc}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})+n^{\text{log}}(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})\big]_{i_{r\phi}\rightarrow i_{r\phi}^{\text{loc}}(\varepsilon,j)+i_{r\phi}^{\text{log}}(\varepsilon,j)}-n^{\text{loc}}(\varepsilon,j)\,.

(102b)

Finally, the hereditary piece is given as a perturbation to the sum of local and logarithmic contributions, namely

\displaystyle n^{\text{hered}}(\varepsilon,j)=\big[n(i_{r\phi},i_{\phi})\big]_{i_{r\phi}\rightarrow i_{r\phi}(\varepsilon,j)}-n^{\text{loc}}(\varepsilon,j)-n^{\text{log}}(\varepsilon,j)\,.

(102c)

I have checked that
n(\varepsilon,j)
agrees at 3PN with Eq. (4.22a) of Ref.
4
. Moreover, I have checked that
n^{\text{loc}}(\varepsilon,j)
exactly reproduces the local 4PN result provided in Eq. (28b) of Ref.
39
.

V.2
Radial period and periastron advance
(P,K)

The radial period
P
and the periastron advance
K
can immediately be deduced from the radial and azimuthal frequencies
(n,\omega)
through the relations
n=2\pi/P
and
\omega=Kn
. The radial period and periastron advance then read

\displaystyle P(\varepsilon,j)

\displaystyle=P^{\text{loc}}(\varepsilon,j)+P^{\text{log}}(\varepsilon,j)+P^{\text{hered}}(\varepsilon,j)\,,

(103a)

\displaystyle K(\varepsilon,j)

\displaystyle=K^{\text{loc}}(\varepsilon,j)+K^{\text{log}}(\varepsilon,j)+K^{\text{hered}}(\varepsilon,j)\,,

(103b)

where the different contributions to
P
and
K
are given, respectively, in Eqs. (
152
) and (
153
).
I have checked that
K(\varepsilon,j)
agrees at 3PN with Eq. (4.22b) of Ref.
4
. I have also verified that
P^{\text{loc}}(\varepsilon,j)
and
K^{\text{loc}}(\varepsilon,j)
exactly reproduce the local 4PN results provided
7
7

7

Eq. (28o) in that reference is also correct, up to a spurious closing square bracket in the expression.
in the Supplemental Material of Ref.
39
.

I also found it instructive to perform the computation in two other ways. The first alternative method, which is only relevant for the
local
piece, does not involve action-angle variables. It consists in integrating Eq. (
29
). The solution reads
30

\displaystyle P^{\text{loc}}=\oint\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{\sqrt{\mathcal{R}(r)}}

and

\displaystyle K^{\text{loc}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\oint\frac{\mathrm{d}r\,\mathcal{S}(r)}{\sqrt{\mathcal{R}(r)}}\,,

(104)

which can be computed
à la Sommerfeld

100
;
44
in terms of
\{A,B,C,D_{n},F,I_{n}\}_{n\in[\![1,7]\!]}
using the integration formula (
38
); see Eq. (
158
) for explicit expressions of
P
and
K
in terms of these coefficients at 4PN.
One then replaces these coefficients by their expressions in terms of energy and angular momentum (
157
), and find perfect agreement with Eqs. (
152
) and (
153
) in the local sector.
The second alternative method (valid for both the local and tail sectors) is by directly varying the radial action
I_{r}
with respect to energy and angular momentum:

\displaystyle P=2\pi\frac{\partial I_{r}}{\partial E}

and

\displaystyle K=-\frac{\partial I_{r}}{\partial J}\,,

(105)

see, e.g., Eqs. (3.6) and (3.7) of Ref.
44
. I checked that this second method recovers exactly the results of Eq. (
103
).

V.3
Dimensionless frequencies
(x,\iota)

Now that these are frequencies computed, it will prove very useful to compute the Blanchet parameters
(x,\iota)
, defined by
30

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{Gm\omega}{c^{3}}\right)^{2/3}

and

\displaystyle\iota

\displaystyle=\frac{3x}{K-1}=\frac{3(Gm\omega)^{2/3}}{c^{2}(\omega/n-1)}\,.

(106)

These definitions are chosen such that
x=\mathcal{O}(1/c^{2})
and
\iota=\mathcal{O}(1)
; recall that
K-1=\mathcal{O}(1/c^{2})
. I thus find that the Blanchet parameters
read
8
8

8

Notice that
\iota(\varepsilon,j)=\iota^{\text{loc}}(\varepsilon,j)+\iota^{\text{log}}(\varepsilon,j)+\iota^{\text{hered}}(\varepsilon,j)
disagrees at 3PN with Eq. (8.23) of Ref.
7
, although they agree at 2PN. The expression in that paper does not correspond to the 3PN local part
\iota^{\text{loc}}(\varepsilon,j)
either. I believe that the discrepancy stems from an order-counting mistake in that work: indeed, the 3PN expression for
\iota
has as much information as the 4PN periastron advance (which was unknown at the time).
To substantiate this claim, I have performed the following check. I computed
K(\varepsilon,j)
using the relation
K=1+3x/\iota
, along with
\iota(\varepsilon,j)
as given by Eq. (8.23) of Ref.
7
and
x(\varepsilon,j)
as given by Eq. (7.10) of Ref.
7
. I then obtained
K^{\text{circ}}(\varepsilon)
using the relation
j^{\text{circ}}(\varepsilon)
given by Eq. (7.12) of Ref.
7
. Finally, I obtained
K^{\text{circ}}(x)
using the relation
\varepsilon^{\text{circ}}(x)
given by Eq. (4.11) of Ref.
14
. This led to a 4PN-accurate expression for
K^{\text{circ}}(x)
, which disagrees with Eq. (5.10) of Ref.
14
. On the contrary, my result agrees in the circular limit with known results; see Sec.
VII
. This mistake propagates into Eq. (8.24) of Ref.
7
, where the instantaneous piece of the 3PN energy flux is expressed in terms of the gauge invariant expressions
(x,\iota)
.

\displaystyle x(\varepsilon,j)

\displaystyle=x^{\text{loc}}(\varepsilon,j)+x^{\text{log}}(\varepsilon,j)+x^{\text{hered}}(\varepsilon,j)\,,

(107a)

\displaystyle\iota(\varepsilon,j)

\displaystyle=\iota^{\text{loc}}(\varepsilon,j)+\iota^{\text{log}}(\varepsilon,j)+\iota^{\text{hered}}(\varepsilon,j)\,,

(107b)

where the different contributions to
x
and
\iota
are given, respectively, in Eqs. (

) and (

).
Finally, these expressions can be inverted to obtain
(\varepsilon,j)
in terms of
(x,\iota)

; this is the main result of this paper. I find that the energy and angular momentum are given in terms of the fundamental frequencies by

\displaystyle\varepsilon(x,\iota)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{\text{loc}}(x,\iota)+\varepsilon^{\text{log}}(x,\iota)+\varepsilon^{\text{hered}}(x,\iota)\,,

(108a)

\displaystyle j(x,\iota)

\displaystyle=j^{\text{loc}}(x,\iota)+j^{\text{log}}(x,\iota)+j^{\text{hered}}(x,\iota)\,,

(108b)

where the local, logarithmic and hereditary pieces are given explicitly by

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\text{loc}}

\displaystyle=x\Biggl\{1+x\Biggl[\frac{5}{4}-\frac{\nu}{12}-\frac{2}{\iota}\Biggr]+x^{2}\Biggl[\frac{5}{8}-\frac{5}{8}\nu-\frac{\nu^{2}}{24}+\frac{5-2\nu}{\sqrt{\iota}}+\frac{1}{\iota}\biggl(-5+\frac{\nu}{3}\biggr)+\frac{5}{\iota^{2}}\Biggr]

\displaystyle+x^{3}\Biggl[-\frac{185}{192}-\frac{75}{64}\nu-\frac{25}{288}\nu^{2}-\frac{35}{5184}\nu^{3}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\iota}}\biggl(\frac{105}{8}-\frac{35}{6}\nu-\frac{7}{6}\nu^{2}\biggr)+\frac{1}{\iota}\biggl(-\frac{15}{4}+\frac{15}{4}\nu+\frac{\nu^{2}}{4}\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\iota^{3/2}}\biggl(-\frac{95}{8}+\nu\Bigl(-\frac{211}{9}+\frac{41}{96}\pi^{2}\Bigr)+\frac{5}{2}\nu^{2}\biggr)+\frac{1}{\iota^{2}}\biggl(\frac{35}{2}-\frac{7}{6}\nu\biggr)-\frac{40}{3\iota^{3}}\Biggr]

\displaystyle+x^{4}\Biggl[-\frac{931}{384}+\frac{245}{1152}\nu+\frac{245}{1152}\nu^{2}+\frac{175}{5184}\nu^{3}+\frac{77}{31104}\nu^{4}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\iota}}\biggl(\frac{1575}{128}-\frac{865}{64}\nu-\frac{65}{32}\nu^{2}-\frac{5}{16}\nu^{3}\biggr)+\frac{1}{\iota}\biggl(\frac{935}{24}-\frac{125}{8}\nu+\frac{205}{36}\nu^{2}+\frac{35}{648}\nu^{3}\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\iota^{3/2}}\biggl(-\frac{3255}{64}+\nu\Bigl(-\frac{26611}{864}-\frac{10969}{9216}\pi^{2}\Bigr)+\nu^{2}\Bigl(-\frac{6757}{432}+\frac{205}{576}\pi^{2}\Bigr)+\frac{65}{24}\nu^{3}\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\iota^{2}}\biggl(\frac{135}{8}-\frac{135}{8}\nu-\frac{9}{8}\nu^{2}\biggr)+\frac{1}{\iota^{5/2}}\biggl(\frac{5723}{128}+\nu\Bigl(\frac{318371}{2880}+\frac{15359}{3072}\pi^{2}\Bigr)+\nu^{2}\Bigl(\frac{679}{32}-\frac{123}{64}\pi^{2}\Bigr)+\frac{55}{16}\nu^{3}\biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\iota^{3}}\biggl(-\frac{175}{3}+\frac{35}{9}\nu\biggr)+\frac{110}{3\iota^{4}}\Biggr]\Biggr\}\,,

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\text{log}}

\displaystyle=\frac{x^{5}\nu}{\iota(1+\sqrt{\iota})}\Biggl\{-\frac{44}{45}-\frac{2588}{45\sqrt{\iota}}-\frac{2752}{45\iota}+\frac{176}{45\iota^{3/2}}+\frac{340}{3\iota^{2}}+\frac{340}{3\iota^{5/2}}

\displaystyle j^{\text{loc}}

\displaystyle=\iota\Biggl\{1+x\Biggl[\frac{5}{12}\nu+\frac{1}{\iota}\biggl(\frac{27}{4}-\frac{5}{2}\nu\biggr)\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\iota^{2}}\biggl(-\frac{455}{32}+\nu\Bigl(\frac{1559}{576}-\frac{98771}{12288}\pi^{2}\Bigr)+\nu^{2}\Bigl(-\frac{5765}{72}+\frac{4715}{1536}\pi^{2}\Bigr)-\frac{85}{32}\nu^{3}\biggr)

\displaystyle j^{\text{log}}

\displaystyle=\frac{x^{3}\sqrt{\iota}\,\nu}{1+\sqrt{\iota}}\Biggl\{\frac{403}{90}+\frac{12421}{270\sqrt{\iota}}-\frac{7567}{135\iota}-\frac{3383}{15\iota^{3/2}}+\frac{3937}{54\iota^{2}}+\frac{11077}{54\iota^{5/2}}

The energy

VI
Redshift invariant

The Detweiler redshift invariant was first introduced in the context of black-hole perturbation theory as the ratio of the rates of change of Schwarzschild coordinate time and of proper time along
a particle’s geodesic in a perturbed and regularized
Schwarzschild metric
53
. It was given a first interpretation as a constant of motion associated with the helical symmetry of spacetime, and a second interpretation as the redshift undergone by light emitted at the particle and reaching an asymptotic observer. It was then shown to be a very useful gauge-invariant quantity for comparison with post-Newtonian theory, where it is defined as
23
;
4
;
76

\displaystyle z_{1}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{-(g_{\alpha\beta})_{1}\frac{v_{1}^{\alpha}v_{1}^{\beta}}{c^{2}}}\,,

(110)

where the 4-vector
v_{1}^{\alpha}=(c,v_{1}^{i})
is associated with the velocity 3-vector
v_{1}^{i}
of the first particle, as well as the regularized value of the metric on the first particle
(g_{\alpha\beta})_{1}
[the regularization procedure was historically Hadamard regularization, then was promoted to dimensional regularization].
Once orbit-averaged, the redshift invariant was shown to be linked, in a variational sense, to the fundamental frequencies
n
and
\omega
, the masses
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
, the energy
E
, the angular momentum
J
, and the radial action
I_{r}
through the
first law of binary black hole mechanics

75
;
76
:

\displaystyle\delta\mathrm{M}=n\ \delta I_{r}+\omega\ \delta L+\langle z_{1}\rangle\,\delta m_{1}+\langle z_{2}\rangle\,\delta m_{2}\,,

(111)

where I have introduced the ADM mass

\displaystyle\mathrm{M}=c^{2}m+E\,.

(112)

This law was shown to hold even in the presence of hereditary effects
29
.
Using this law, one finds that the redshift invariant can also be obtained directly by differentiating the expression of the energy in terms of action-angle variables with respect to the masses of the two compact objects. However, one should now be careful with the scalings of various quantities with the masses
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
.
Using Eqs. (
95
) and (
51
), one can express the ADM mass (
112
) in terms of the dimensionful action variables
(I_{r},I_{\phi}=J)
and the masses
(m_{1},m_{2})
of each particle.
The averaged redshift invariant (with respect to particle
1
) is then simply given by
75
;
21
;
29
;
76

\displaystyle z_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial M}{\partial m_{1}}\Bigg|_{m_{2},I_{r},J}\,.

(113)

Of course, the initial result after differentiation is expressed in terms of the action variables, and one can then use Eq. (
97a
) and Sec.
I.1
to re-express it in terms of
(m,\nu,\delta,\varepsilon,j)
.

I finally find that the redshift invariant is given in terms of energy and angular momentum by

\displaystyle\langle z_{1}\rangle(\varepsilon,j)=\langle z_{1}^{\text{loc}}\rangle(\varepsilon,j)+\langle z_{1}^{\text{log}}\rangle(\varepsilon,j)+\langle z_{1}^{\text{hered}}\rangle(\varepsilon,j)\,,

(114)

where the local, logarithmic and hereditary pieces read

\displaystyle\langle z_{1}^{\text{loc}}\rangle

\displaystyle=1+\varepsilon\Biggl\{-\frac{3}{4}+\frac{\nu}{2}+\frac{3}{4}\delta\Biggr\}+\varepsilon^{2}\Biggl\{\frac{15}{8}-\frac{3}{16}\nu+\frac{\nu^{2}}{4}+\delta\left(-\frac{15}{8}+\frac{3}{16}\nu\right)+\frac{-3+3\delta}{\sqrt{j}}\Biggr\}

\displaystyle\qquad+\varepsilon^{3}\Biggl\{-\frac{65}{16}-\frac{3}{32}\nu^{2}+\frac{\nu^{3}}{8}+\delta\left(\frac{65}{16}+\frac{3}{32}\nu^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{j}}\Biggl[\frac{105}{8}-\frac{33}{8}\nu+3\nu^{2}+\delta\left(-\frac{105}{8}+\frac{33}{8}\nu\right)\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{j^{3/2}}\Biggl[-\frac{35}{2}+\frac{25}{4}\nu-5\nu^{2}+\delta\left(\frac{35}{2}-\frac{25}{4}\nu\right)\Biggr]\Biggr\}

\displaystyle\qquad+\varepsilon^{4}\Biggl\{\frac{291}{32}+\frac{65}{64}\nu+\frac{15}{128}\nu^{2}-\frac{3}{64}\nu^{3}+\frac{\nu^{4}}{16}+\delta\left(-\frac{291}{32}-\frac{65}{64}\nu-\frac{15}{128}\nu^{2}+\frac{3}{64}\nu^{3}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{\sqrt{j}}\Biggl[-\frac{5625}{128}+\frac{1125}{64}\nu-\frac{1749}{128}\nu^{2}+\frac{33}{8}\nu^{3}+\delta\left(\frac{5625}{128}-\frac{1125}{64}\nu+\frac{549}{128}\nu^{2}\right)\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{j}\Biggl[\frac{45}{2}-9\nu+\delta\left(-\frac{45}{2}+9\nu\right)\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{j^{3/2}}\Biggl[\frac{1785}{16}+\nu\left(-\frac{13543}{96}+\frac{287}{256}\pi^{2}\right)+\nu^{2}\left(\frac{9391}{96}-\frac{41}{64}\pi^{2}\right)-\frac{125}{8}\nu^{3}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\delta\left[-\frac{1785}{16}+\nu\left(\frac{13543}{96}-\frac{287}{256}\pi^{2}\right)-\frac{505}{32}\nu^{2}\right]\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{j^{5/2}}\Biggl[-\frac{693}{4}+\nu\left(\frac{875}{4}-\frac{861}{256}\pi^{2}\right)+\nu^{2}\left(-\frac{271}{2}+\frac{123}{64}\pi^{2}\right)+\frac{21}{2}\nu^{3}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\delta\left[\frac{693}{4}+\nu\left(-\frac{875}{4}+\frac{861}{256}\pi^{2}\right)+\frac{21}{2}\nu^{2}\right]\Biggr]\Biggr\}

\displaystyle\qquad+\varepsilon^{5}\Biggl\{-\frac{1293}{64}-\frac{291}{64}\nu-\frac{65}{256}\nu^{2}+\frac{15}{256}\nu^{3}-\frac{3}{128}\nu^{4}+\frac{\nu^{5}}{32}+\delta\left(\frac{1293}{64}+\frac{291}{64}\nu+\frac{65}{256}\nu^{2}-\frac{15}{256}\nu^{3}+\frac{3}{128}\nu^{4}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{\sqrt{j}}\Biggl[\frac{133485}{1024}-\frac{52215}{1024}\nu+\frac{39315}{1024}\nu^{2}-\frac{14697}{1024}\nu^{3}+\frac{525}{128}\nu^{4}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\delta\left(-\frac{133485}{1024}+\frac{52215}{1024}\nu-\frac{17835}{1024}\nu^{2}+\frac{3897}{1024}\nu^{3}\right)\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{j}\Biggl[-\frac{1215}{8}+\frac{405}{4}\nu-45\nu^{2}+9\nu^{3}+\delta\left(\frac{1215}{8}-\frac{405}{4}\nu+\frac{45}{2}\nu^{2}\right)\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{j^{3/2}}\Biggl[-\frac{107625}{256}+\nu\left(\frac{376279}{512}-\frac{160827}{16384}\pi^{2}\right)+\nu^{2}\left(-\frac{10703}{18}+\frac{78127}{12288}\pi^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\nu^{3}\left(\frac{379681}{1536}-\frac{943}{512}\pi^{2}\right)-\frac{3415}{128}\nu^{4}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\delta\left[\frac{107625}{256}+\nu\left(-\frac{376279}{512}+\frac{160827}{16384}\pi^{2}\right)+\nu^{2}\left(\frac{108451}{384}-\frac{4633}{2048}\pi^{2}\right)-\frac{12515}{512}\nu^{3}\right]\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{j^{2}}\Biggl[\frac{1155}{4}+\nu\left(-\frac{2539}{8}+\frac{123}{64}\pi^{2}\right)+\frac{315}{4}\nu^{2}-15\nu^{3}+\delta\left[-\frac{1155}{4}+\nu\left(\frac{2539}{8}-\frac{123}{64}\pi^{2}\right)-\frac{165}{4}\nu^{2}\right]\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{j^{5/2}}\Biggl[\frac{46431}{32}+\nu\left(-\frac{1018419}{320}+\frac{514611}{8192}\pi^{2}\right)+\nu^{2}\left(\frac{311969}{120}-\frac{3095}{64}\pi^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\nu^{3}\left(-\frac{30379}{32}+\frac{7995}{512}\pi^{2}\right)+\frac{651}{16}\nu^{4}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\delta\left[-\frac{46431}{32}+\nu\left(\frac{1018419}{320}-\frac{514611}{8192}\pi^{2}\right)+\nu^{2}\left(-\frac{18285}{16}+\frac{40221}{2048}\pi^{2}\right)+\frac{1197}{32}\nu^{3}\right]\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{j^{7/2}}\Biggl[-\frac{32175}{16}+\nu\left(\frac{248057}{64}-\frac{1275315}{16384}\pi^{2}\right)+\nu^{2}\left(-\frac{779989}{288}+\frac{671105}{12288}\pi^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\nu^{3}\left(\frac{77185}{96}-\frac{1025}{64}\pi^{2}\right)-\frac{135}{8}\nu^{4}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\delta\left[\frac{32175}{16}+\nu\left(-\frac{248057}{64}+\frac{1275315}{16384}\pi^{2}\right)+\nu^{2}\left(\frac{94625}{96}-\frac{5125}{256}\pi^{2}\right)-\frac{495}{32}\nu^{3}\right]\Biggr]\Biggr\}\,,

(115a)

\displaystyle\langle z_{1}^{\text{log}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon^{5}\nu}{j^{3/2}}\Biggl\{-\frac{617}{60}+\frac{17}{3}\nu+\frac{617}{60}\delta+\frac{1}{\sqrt{j}}\biggl[-\frac{673}{5}+\frac{2692}{45}\nu+\frac{673}{5}\delta\biggr]+\frac{1}{j}\biggl[\frac{1907}{10}-\frac{478}{5}\nu-\frac{1907}{10}\delta\biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{j^{3/2}}\biggl[255-\frac{340}{3}\nu-255\delta\biggr]+\frac{1}{j^{2}}\biggl[-\frac{3533}{12}+\frac{1291}{9}\nu+\frac{3533}{12}\delta\biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\Biggl(\frac{111}{10}-\frac{74}{15}\nu-\frac{111}{10}\delta+\frac{1}{j^{2}}\biggl[\frac{255}{2}-\frac{170}{3}\nu-\frac{255}{2}\delta\biggr]+\frac{1}{j}\biggl[-\frac{549}{5}+\frac{244}{5}\nu+\frac{549}{5}\delta\biggr]\Biggr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\left(\ln\varepsilon-2\ln\left(\frac{1+\sqrt{j}}{8j}\right)+2\gamma_{\text{E}}\right)\Biggr\}\,,

(115b)

\displaystyle\langle z_{1}^{\text{hered }}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon^{5}\nu}{j^{3/2}}\Biggl\{\ln j\left(-\frac{333}{10}+\frac{74}{5}\nu+\frac{333}{10}\delta+\frac{1}{j}\bigg[\frac{1647}{5}-\frac{732}{5}\nu-\frac{1647}{5}\delta\bigg]+\frac{1}{j^{2}}\bigg[-\frac{765}{2}+170\nu+\frac{765}{2}\delta\bigg]\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\lambda_{0}\left(\sqrt{1-j}\right)\Biggl(\frac{1}{j}\biggl[\frac{432}{5}-\frac{192}{5}\nu-\frac{432}{5}\delta\biggr]+\frac{1}{j^{2}}\biggl[-\frac{432}{5}+\frac{192}{5}\nu+\frac{432}{5}\delta\biggr]\Biggr)\Biggr\}\,.

(115c)

I have compared the previous expression with
\langle U_{1}\rangle=1/\langle z_{1}\rangle
given in Eq. (4.39) of Ref.
4
in terms of
(\varepsilon,j)
and found perfect agreement
9
9

9

Agreement is found after accounting for the fact that Refs.
4
;
19
;
29
use a different convention for the relative mass than me: they assume that
m_{1}\geq m_{2}
and define
\Delta\equiv(m_{2}-m_{1})/m=-\delta
.

at 3PN. Moreover, I have explicitly checked that the ‘first integral’ relation stemming from the first law of black hole mechanics, given by Ref. (3.8) of
76
, explicitly holds through 4PN order, including the tail term. This relation reads in my notations

\displaystyle m+\frac{E}{c^{2}}=m_{1}\langle z_{1}\rangle+m_{2}\langle z_{2}\rangle+\frac{2}{c^{2}}\Big(\omega\,J+n\,I_{r}\Big)\,.

(116)

To perform this check, I have written
(m_{1},m_{2})
in terms of
(m,\nu,\delta)
and expressed all quantities in terms of
(\varepsilon,j)
using Eqs. (
97b
), (
101
), (
114
) and the definitions of Sec.
I.1
; recall also that
\langle z_{2}\rangle
is immediately obtained from
\langle z_{1}\rangle
by performing the operation
\delta\rightarrow-\delta
.

One now computes the redshift in terms of the Blanchet parameters
(x,\iota)
, which reads

\displaystyle\langle z_{1}\rangle(x,\iota)=\langle z_{1}^{\text{loc}}\rangle(x,\iota)+\langle z_{1}^{\text{log}}\rangle(x,\iota)+\langle z_{1}^{\text{hered}}\rangle(x,\iota)\,.

(117)

One should keep in mind the discussion in Sec.
IV.1
for the definition of this split; namely, recall that, e.g.,
z_{1}^{\text{hered}}(x,\iota)\neq z_{1}^{\text{hered}}\Bigl(\varepsilon(x,\iota),j(x,\iota)\Bigr)
. I find that the local, logarithmic and hereditary pieces are given by

\displaystyle\langle z_{1}^{\text{loc}}\rangle

\displaystyle=1+x\Biggl\{-\frac{3}{4}+\frac{\nu}{2}+\frac{3}{4}\delta\Biggr\}+x^{2}\Biggl\{\frac{15}{16}+\frac{\nu}{2}+\frac{5}{24}\nu^{2}+\delta\left(-\frac{15}{16}+\frac{\nu}{8}\right)+\frac{-3+3\delta}{\sqrt{\iota}}+\frac{1}{\iota}\Biggl[\frac{3}{2}-\frac{3}{2}\delta-\nu\Biggr]\Biggr\}

\displaystyle\qquad+x^{3}\Biggl\{\frac{5}{32}+\frac{9}{32}\nu^{2}+\frac{\nu^{3}}{16}+\delta\left(-\frac{5}{32}+\frac{5}{16}\nu+\frac{\nu^{2}}{32}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\iota}}\Biggl[\frac{15}{8}+\nu+2\nu^{2}+\delta\left(-\frac{15}{8}+\frac{3}{2}\nu\right)\Biggr]+\frac{1}{\iota}\Biggl[-\frac{15}{4}-2\nu-\frac{5}{6}\nu^{2}+\delta\left(\frac{15}{4}-\frac{\nu}{2}\right)\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{\iota^{3/2}}\Biggl[\frac{37}{8}+\frac{5}{2}\nu-5\nu^{2}+\delta\left(-\frac{37}{8}-\frac{5}{2}\nu\right)\Biggr]+\frac{1}{\iota^{2}}\Biggl[-\frac{15}{4}+\frac{5}{2}\nu+\frac{15}{4}\delta\Biggr]\Biggr\}

\displaystyle\qquad+x^{4}\Biggl\{-\frac{37}{256}-\frac{5}{6}\nu+\frac{5}{64}\nu^{2}+\frac{49}{864}\nu^{3}+\frac{91}{10368}\nu^{4}+\delta\left(\frac{37}{256}+\frac{45}{128}\nu+\frac{5}{128}\nu^{2}+\frac{7}{1728}\nu^{3}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\iota}}\Biggl[\frac{315}{128}+\frac{35}{8}\nu+\frac{77}{96}\nu^{2}+\frac{7}{6}\nu^{3}+\delta\left(-\frac{315}{128}+\frac{35}{16}\nu+\frac{21}{32}\nu^{2}\right)\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{\iota}\Biggl[-\frac{15}{16}-\frac{27}{16}\nu^{2}-\frac{3}{8}\nu^{3}+\delta\left(\frac{15}{16}-\frac{15}{8}\nu-\frac{3}{16}\nu^{2}\right)\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{\iota^{3/2}}\Biggl[-\frac{285}{64}+\nu\left(-\frac{1129}{48}+\frac{41}{128}\pi^{2}\right)+\nu^{2}\left(\frac{3781}{144}-\frac{41}{96}\pi^{2}\right)-\frac{25}{4}\nu^{3}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\delta\left[\frac{285}{64}+\nu\left(\frac{211}{12}-\frac{41}{128}\pi^{2}\right)-\frac{45}{16}\nu^{2}\right]\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{\iota^{2}}\Biggl[\frac{105}{8}+7\nu+\frac{35}{12}\nu^{2}+\delta\left(-\frac{105}{8}+\frac{7}{4}\nu\right)\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{\iota^{5/2}}\Biggl[-\frac{1797}{128}+\nu\left(\frac{355}{16}-\frac{123}{128}\pi^{2}\right)+\nu^{2}\left(-\frac{1321}{32}+\frac{123}{64}\pi^{2}\right)-\frac{33}{4}\nu^{3}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\delta\left[\frac{1797}{128}+\nu\left(-\frac{355}{16}+\frac{123}{128}\pi^{2}\right)-\frac{99}{32}\nu^{2}\right]\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{\iota^{3}}\Biggl[10-\frac{20}{3}\nu-10\delta\Biggr]\Biggr\}

\displaystyle\qquad+x^{5}\Biggl\{-\frac{133}{512}-\frac{7}{6}\nu+\frac{385}{4608}\nu^{2}-\frac{425}{6912}\nu^{3}-\frac{1045}{41472}\nu^{4}-\frac{187}{62208}\nu^{5}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\delta\left(\frac{133}{512}-\frac{35}{768}\nu-\frac{35}{512}\nu^{2}-\frac{25}{1728}\nu^{3}-\frac{55}{41472}\nu^{4}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\iota}}\Biggl[\frac{1575}{1024}+\frac{355}{128}\nu-\frac{195}{256}\nu^{2}+\frac{55}{64}\nu^{3}+\frac{5}{16}\nu^{4}+\delta\left(-\frac{1575}{1024}+\frac{865}{256}\nu+\frac{195}{256}\nu^{2}+\frac{5}{32}\nu^{3}\right)\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{\iota}\Biggl[\frac{187}{32}+\frac{355}{24}\nu+\frac{33}{8}\nu^{2}-\frac{427}{108}\nu^{3}-\frac{91}{1296}\nu^{4}+\delta\left(-\frac{187}{32}+\frac{75}{16}\nu-\frac{41}{16}\nu^{2}-\frac{7}{216}\nu^{3}\right)\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{\iota^{3/2}}\Biggl[-\frac{9765}{1024}+\nu\left(-\frac{85201}{2304}-\frac{10969}{24576}\pi^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\nu^{2}\left(-\frac{7591}{6912}+\frac{18349}{36864}\pi^{2}\right)+\nu^{3}\left(\frac{15269}{864}-\frac{205}{576}\pi^{2}\right)-\frac{455}{96}\nu^{4}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\delta\left[\frac{9765}{1024}+\nu\left(\frac{26611}{2304}+\frac{10969}{24576}\pi^{2}\right)+\nu^{2}\left(\frac{6757}{768}-\frac{205}{1024}\pi^{2}\right)-\frac{65}{32}\nu^{3}\right]\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{\iota^{2}}\Biggl[\frac{135}{32}+\frac{243}{32}\nu^{2}+\frac{27}{16}\nu^{3}+\delta\left(-\frac{135}{32}+\frac{135}{16}\nu+\frac{27}{32}\nu^{2}\right)\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{\iota^{5/2}}\Biggl[\frac{17169}{1024}+\nu\left(\frac{50527}{480}+\frac{15359}{4096}\pi^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\nu^{2}\left(-\frac{998489}{11520}-\frac{22001}{3072}\pi^{2}\right)+\nu^{3}\left(-\frac{3065}{64}+\frac{615}{128}\pi^{2}\right)-\frac{55}{4}\nu^{4}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\delta\left[-\frac{17169}{1024}+\nu\left(-\frac{318371}{3840}-\frac{15359}{4096}\pi^{2}\right)+\nu^{2}\left(-\frac{6111}{256}+\frac{1107}{512}\pi^{2}\right)-\frac{165}{32}\nu^{3}\right]\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{\iota^{3}}\Biggl[-\frac{175}{4}-\frac{70}{3}\nu-\frac{175}{18}\nu^{2}+\delta\left(\frac{175}{4}-\frac{35}{6}\nu\right)\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{\iota^{7/2}}\Biggl[\frac{46653}{1024}+\nu\left(-\frac{662129}{2304}+\frac{162343}{24576}\pi^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\nu^{2}\left(\frac{1532149}{2304}-\frac{199243}{12288}\pi^{2}\right)+\left(-\frac{11167}{48}+\frac{1025}{128}\pi^{2}\right)\nu^{3}-\frac{765}{32}\nu^{4}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\delta\left[-\frac{46653}{1024}+\nu\left(\frac{662129}{2304}-\frac{162343}{24576}\pi^{2}\right)+\nu^{2}\left(-\frac{23099}{256}+\frac{3075}{1024}\pi^{2}\right)-\frac{255}{32}\nu^{3}\right]\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{\iota^{4}}\Biggl[-\frac{55}{2}+\frac{55}{3}\nu+\frac{55}{2}\delta\Biggr]\Biggr\}\,,

(118a)

\displaystyle\langle z_{1}^{\text{log}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{x^{5}\nu}{\left(1+\sqrt{\iota}\right)\iota}\Biggl\{-\frac{17}{6}+\frac{233}{45}\nu+\frac{17}{6}\delta+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\iota}}\Biggl[-\frac{2947}{90}+\frac{551}{15}\nu+\frac{2947}{90}\delta\Biggr]+\frac{1}{\iota}\Biggl[\frac{161}{9}-\frac{2986}{45}\nu-\frac{161}{9}\delta\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\frac{1}{\iota^{3/2}}\Biggl[\frac{1567}{15}-\frac{9314}{45}\nu-\frac{1567}{15}\delta\Biggr]+\frac{1}{\iota^{2}}\Biggl[-\frac{271}{18}+\frac{781}{9}\nu+\frac{271}{18}\delta\Biggr]+\frac{1}{\iota^{5/2}}\Biggl[-\frac{1291}{18}+\frac{1801}{9}\nu+\frac{1291}{18}\delta\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\left(\ln x-2\ln\left(\frac{1+\sqrt{\iota}}{8\iota}\right)+2\gamma_{\text{E}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad\times\Biggl(\frac{37}{15}-\frac{74}{45}\nu-\frac{37}{15}\delta+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\iota}}\Biggl[\frac{37}{15}-\frac{74}{45}\nu-\frac{37}{15}\delta\Biggr]+\frac{1}{\iota}\Biggl[-\frac{122}{5}+\frac{488}{15}\nu+\frac{122}{5}\delta\Biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+\frac{1}{\iota^{3/2}}\Biggl[-\frac{122}{5}+\frac{488}{15}\nu+\frac{122}{5}\delta\Biggr]+\frac{1}{\iota^{2}}\Biggl[\frac{85}{3}-\frac{170}{3}\nu-\frac{85}{3}\delta\Biggr]+\frac{1}{\iota^{5/2}}\Biggl[\frac{85}{3}-\frac{170}{3}\nu-\frac{85}{3}\delta\Biggr]\Biggr)\Biggr\}\,,

(118b)

\displaystyle\langle z_{1}^{\text{hered}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{x^{5}\nu}{\iota^{3/2}}\Biggl\{-\frac{74\nu}{15}+\frac{244\nu}{5\iota}-\frac{170\nu}{3\iota^{2}}+\ln\iota\left(-\frac{37}{5}+\frac{37}{5}\delta+\frac{1}{\iota}\Biggl[\frac{366}{5}-\frac{488}{5}\nu-\frac{366}{5}\delta\Biggr]+\frac{-85+170\nu+85\delta}{\iota^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\lambda_{0}\!\left(\sqrt{1-\iota}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\iota}\Biggl[\frac{96}{5}-\frac{128}{5}\nu-\frac{96}{5}\delta\Biggr]+\frac{1}{\iota^{2}}\Biggl[-\frac{96}{5}+\frac{192}{5}\nu+\frac{96}{5}\delta\Biggr]\right)+\frac{32\nu}{5\iota}\sqrt{1-\iota}\ \lambda_{0}^{\prime}\!\left(\sqrt{1-\iota}\right)\Biggr\}\,.

(118c)

I have found that this expression is in perfect agreement
9
with Eq. (49) of Ref.
19
at 3PN.

Finally, I compared these results with the gravitational self-force literature at both geodesic order and first self-force (1SF) orders. For that, I follow the notations of Ref.
66
and define new variables
(y,\lambda)
, which are similar to the Blanchet parameters
(x,\iota)
, except for the fact that they are normalized with respect to the ‘primary mass’
m_{1}
rather than the total mass
m
. Namely, they are defined from the frequencies
(n,\omega)
as

\displaystyle y=\left(\frac{Gm_{1}\omega}{c^{3}}\right)^{2/3}

and

\displaystyle\lambda=\frac{3y}{\omega/n-1}\ .

(119)

Introducing the ‘small’ mass ratio
\epsilon=m_{2}/m_{1}\leq 1
, it is immediate to obtain the relations

\displaystyle m=m_{1}(1+\epsilon)\,,

\displaystyle\nu=\frac{\epsilon}{(1+\epsilon)^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\delta=\frac{1-\epsilon}{1+\epsilon}\,,

\displaystyle x=y(1+\epsilon)^{2/3}\,,

and

\displaystyle\iota=\lambda(1+\epsilon)^{2/3}\,.

(120)

One then performs the small mass ratio expansion in
\epsilon\ll 1
of the redshift associated with the ‘secondary object’ of mass
m_{2}
, and replace the variables
(y,\lambda)
by their 4PN-accurate expressions in terms of the Darwin variables
(p,e)
; see (
147
) and (
147
). The result then has the structure

\displaystyle\langle z_{2}\rangle(p,e)=\langle z^{\text{geo}}_{2}\rangle+\epsilon\,\langle z^{\text{1SF}}_{2}\rangle+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})\,.

(121)

I find that the geodesic piece
\langle z^{\text{geo}}_{2}\rangle
is in perfect agreement with the prediction from black hole perturbation theory; see Eq. (
147
). I then compared the 1SF piece
\langle z^{\text{1SF}}_{2}\rangle=-\langle u_{2}^{\text{1SF}}\rangle\times\langle z_{2}^{\text{geo}}\rangle^{2}
against Eq. (5.5) of Ref.
85
; see also Eq. (5.1) of Ref.
66
. Once the enhancement function (
77
) has been identified with Eq. (5.10) of Ref.
85
, I find perfect agreement at 4PN for arbitrary eccentricity. This latter agreement is probably the most stringent test of my results, because it is the only test that probes the effects of the tail term at 4PN on orbits with arbitrary eccentricity.

VII
Reductions to circular orbits

In order to validate these results, it is instructive to take the limit of circular orbits. This is defined gauge-invariantly by the condition that the radial action vanishes, namely
i_{r}(\varepsilon,j)=0
. This defines a ‘circular link’, namely a relation between energy and angular momentum that must hold for circular orbits; this link is denoted by
j_{\text{circ}}(\varepsilon)
. Crucially, one needs to account for the fact that the radial action has both local and tail contributions, see Eq. (
97b
). If one were to ignore the tail sector [namely setting
i_{r}^{\text{loc}}=0
using Eq. (
III.1
)], one would find the ‘local’ relation

\displaystyle j^{\text{loc}}_{\text{circ}}

\displaystyle=1+\varepsilon\left(\frac{9}{4}+\frac{\nu}{4}\right)+\varepsilon^{2}\left(\frac{81}{16}-2\nu+\frac{\nu^{2}}{16}\right)+\varepsilon^{3}\left(\frac{945}{64}+\nu\left[-\frac{7699}{192}+\frac{41}{32}\pi^{2}\right]+\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}+\frac{\nu^{3}}{64}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\varepsilon^{4}\left(\frac{14337}{256}+\nu\left[-\frac{218431}{1920}+\frac{677}{512}\pi^{2}\right]+\nu^{2}\left[\frac{229}{4}-\frac{123}{64}\pi^{2}\right]+\frac{\nu^{4}}{256}\right)\,.

(122a)

Including the tail pieces then leads to the extra contributions

\displaystyle j^{\text{log}}_{\text{circ}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{64}{5}\varepsilon^{4}\nu\Bigl[\ln(16\,\varepsilon)+2\gamma_{\text{E}}\Bigr]\,,

\displaystyle j^{\text{hered}}_{\text{circ}}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(122b)

Thus, the full relation
j_{\text{circ}}=j^{\text{loc}}_{\text{circ}}+j^{\text{log}}_{\text{circ}}+j^{\text{hered}}_{\text{circ}}
is given by

\displaystyle j_{\text{circ}}

\displaystyle=1+\varepsilon\left(\frac{9}{4}+\frac{\nu}{4}\right)+\varepsilon^{2}\left(\frac{81}{16}-2\nu+\frac{\nu^{2}}{16}\right)+\varepsilon^{3}\left(\frac{945}{64}+\nu\left[-\frac{7699}{192}+\frac{41\pi^{2}}{32}\right]+\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}+\frac{\nu^{3}}{64}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\varepsilon^{4}\left(\frac{14337}{256}+\nu\left[-\frac{218431}{1920}-\frac{128}{5}\gamma_{\text{E}}+\frac{677}{512}\pi^{2}-\frac{64}{5}\ln(16\,\varepsilon)\right]+\left[\frac{229}{4}-\frac{123}{64}\pi^{2}\right]\nu^{2}+\frac{\nu^{4}}{256}\right)\,.

(123)

This expression agrees with Eq. (4.43) of Ref.
4
at 3PN. More importantly, I have checked that this result is in perfect agreement at 4PN with Eq. (5.3) of
46
, once the adequate map between variables is performed.

I then proceed to translate this into a circular link between
x
and
\iota
. Recall that we actually lose a PN order due to the fact that the radial and azimuthal frequencies are degenerate at Newtonian order.
8
Thus, restricting to the local section, I find that the local, 3PN, circular link reads

\displaystyle\iota_{\text{circ}}^{\text{loc}}

\displaystyle=1+x\left(-\frac{9}{2}+\frac{7}{3}\nu\right)+x^{2}\left(-\frac{9}{4}+\nu\left[\frac{397}{12}-\frac{41}{32}\pi^{2}\right]+\frac{28}{9}\nu^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+x^{3}\left(-\frac{27}{4}+\nu\left[-\frac{36943}{216}+\frac{58265}{9216}\pi^{2}\right]+\nu^{2}\left[\frac{445}{12}-\frac{41}{32}\pi^{2}\right]+\frac{245}{81}\nu^{3}\right)\,.

(124a)

This expression immediately converts to the 4PN local periastron advance

\displaystyle K_{\text{circ}}^{\text{loc}}

\displaystyle=1+3x+x^{2}\left(\frac{27}{2}-7\nu\right)+x^{3}\left(\frac{135}{2}+\nu\left[-\frac{649}{4}+\frac{123}{32}\pi^{2}\right]+7\nu^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+x^{4}\left(\frac{2835}{8}+\nu\left[-\frac{60257}{72}+\frac{48007}{3072}\pi^{2}\right]+\nu^{2}\left[\frac{5861}{12}-\frac{451}{32}\pi^{2}\right]-\frac{98}{27}\nu^{3}\right)\,,

(124b)

which perfectly agrees with the local 4PN periastron advance for circular orbits obtained in Eq. (5.9) of Ref.
14
.
One then computes the tail pieces, which read

\displaystyle\iota_{\text{circ}}^{\text{log}}

\displaystyle=x^{3}\nu\bigg\{\frac{1256}{45}\Bigl[\ln(16x)+2\gamma_{\text{E}}\Bigr]-\frac{352}{15}\bigg\}\,,

\displaystyle\iota_{\text{circ}}^{\text{hered}}

\displaystyle=x^{3}\nu\bigg\{-\frac{4432}{45}\ln 2+\frac{486}{5}\ln 3\bigg\}\,,

(124c)

\displaystyle K_{\text{circ}}^{\text{log}}

\displaystyle=x^{4}\nu\bigg\{-\frac{1256}{15}\Bigl[\ln(16x)+2\gamma_{\text{E}}\Bigr]+\frac{352}{5}\bigg\}\,,

\displaystyle K_{\text{circ}}^{\text{hered}}

\displaystyle=x^{4}\nu\bigg\{\frac{4432}{15}\ln 2+\frac{1458}{5}\ln 3\bigg\}\,.

(124d)

One now adds everything together, namely
\iota_{\text{circ}}=\iota_{\text{circ}}^{\text{loc}}+\iota_{\text{circ}}^{\text{log}}+\iota_{\text{circ}}^{\text{hered}}
and
K_{\text{circ}}=K_{\text{circ}}^{\text{loc}}+K_{\text{circ}}^{\text{log}}+K_{\text{circ}}^{\text{hered}}
, and finds

\displaystyle\iota_{\text{circ}}

\displaystyle=1+x\left(-\frac{9}{2}+\frac{7}{3}\nu\right)+x^{2}\left(-\frac{9}{4}+\nu\left[\frac{397}{12}-\frac{41}{32}\pi^{2}\right]+\frac{28}{9}\nu^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\!+x^{3}\Biggl(-\frac{27}{4}+\nu\left[-\frac{210059}{1080}+\frac{2512}{45}\gamma_{\text{E}}+\frac{58265}{9216}\pi^{2}-\frac{592}{45}\ln 2+\frac{486}{5}\ln 3+\frac{1256}{45}\ln x\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\ \quad\qquad+\nu^{2}\left[\frac{445}{12}-\frac{41}{32}\pi^{2}\right]+\frac{245}{81}\nu^{3}\Biggr)\,,

(125a)

\displaystyle K_{\text{circ}}

\displaystyle=1+3x+x^{2}\left(\frac{27}{2}-7\nu\right)+x^{3}\left(\frac{135}{2}+\nu\left[-\frac{649}{4}+\frac{123}{32}\pi^{2}\right]+7\nu^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\!+x^{4}\Biggl(\frac{2835}{8}+\nu\left[-\frac{275941}{360}-\frac{2512}{15}\gamma_{\text{E}}+\frac{48007}{3072}\pi^{2}-\frac{592}{15}\ln 2-\frac{1458}{5}\ln 3-\frac{1256}{15}\ln x\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad\qquad+\nu^{2}\left[\frac{5861}{12}-\frac{451}{32}\pi^{2}\right]-\frac{98}{27}\nu^{3}\Biggr)\,.

(125b)

Notably, the latter result is in perfect agreement with the 4PN periastron advance for circular orbits given in Eq. (5.10) of Ref.
14
. I have also found perfect agreement with Eq. (5.4b) of Ref.
46
, once the appropriate variable conversions have been performed.

Finally, the 4PN redshift can also be reduced to circular orbits; in this case, the angular brackets that denote orbit averaging can be dropped. It can first be obtained in terms of the energy
\varepsilon
thanks to the relation
j_{\text{circ}}(\varepsilon)
given in Eq. (
VII
). The redshift on circular orbits in terms of
\varepsilon
then reads

\displaystyle z_{1}^{\text{circ}}=1

\displaystyle+\varepsilon\Biggl\{-\frac{3}{4}+\frac{\nu}{2}+\frac{3}{4}\delta\Biggr\}+\varepsilon^{2}\Biggl\{-\frac{9}{8}-\frac{3}{16}\nu+\frac{\nu^{2}}{4}+\delta\left(\frac{9}{8}+\frac{3}{16}\nu\right)\Biggr\}

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{3}\Biggl\{-\frac{81}{16}+\frac{5}{2}\nu-\frac{67}{32}\nu^{2}+\frac{\nu^{3}}{8}+\delta\left(\frac{81}{16}-\frac{5}{2}\nu+\frac{3}{32}\nu^{2}\right)\Biggr\}

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{4}\Biggl\{-\frac{891}{32}+\nu\left(\frac{13723}{192}-\frac{287}{128}\pi^{2}\right)+\nu^{2}\left(-\frac{15179}{384}+\frac{41}{32}\pi^{2}\right)+\frac{29}{64}\nu^{3}+\frac{\nu^{4}}{16}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\delta\left[\frac{891}{32}+\nu\left(-\frac{13723}{192}+\frac{287}{128}\pi^{2}\right)+\frac{89}{128}\nu^{2}+\frac{3}{64}\nu^{3}\right]\Biggr\}

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{5}\Biggl\{-\frac{10935}{64}+\nu\left(\frac{118349}{320}+\frac{288}{5}\gamma_{\text{E}}-\frac{11997}{2048}\pi^{2}+\frac{144}{5}\ln(16 \,\varepsilon)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\nu^{2}\left(-\frac{965807}{3840}+\frac{2891}{512}\pi^{2}-\frac{128}{5}\gamma_{\text{E}}-\frac{64}{5}\ln(16\, \varepsilon)\right)+\nu^{3}\left(\frac{76189}{768}-\frac{205}{64}\pi^{2}\right)-\frac{19}{128}\nu^{4}+\frac{\nu^{5}}{32}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\delta\Biggl[\frac{10935}{64}+\nu\left(-\frac{118349}{320}+\frac{11997}{2048}\pi^{2}-\frac{288}{5}\gamma_{\text{E}}-\frac{144}{5}\ln(16\, \varepsilon)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\nu^{2}\left(\frac{34561}{256}-\frac{1107}{256}\pi^{2}\right)-\frac{31}{256}\nu^{3}+\frac{3}{128}\nu^{4}\Biggr]\Biggr\}\,.

(126a)

It can alternatively be expressed in terms of
x
thanks to the relation
\iota_{\text{circ}}(x)
given in Eq. (
125
). It reads

\displaystyle z_{1}^{\text{circ}}

\displaystyle=1+x\Biggl\{-\frac{3}{4}+\frac{\nu}{2}+\frac{3}{4}\delta\Biggr\}+x^{2}\Biggl\{-\frac{9}{16}-\frac{\nu}{2}+\frac{5}{24}\nu^{2}+\delta\left(\frac{9}{16}+\frac{\nu}{8}\right)\Biggr\}

\displaystyle+x^{3}\Biggl\{-\frac{27}{32}-\frac{\nu}{2}-\frac{39}{32}\nu^{2}+\frac{\nu^{3}}{16}+\delta\left(\frac{27}{32}-\frac{19}{16}\nu+\frac{\nu^{2}}{32}\right)\Biggr\}

\displaystyle+x^{4}\Biggl\{-\frac{405}{256}+\nu\left(\frac{38}{3}-\frac{41}{64}\pi^{2}\right)+\nu^{2}\left(-\frac{3863}{576}+\frac{41}{192}\pi^{2}\right)+\frac{973}{864}\nu^{3}+\frac{91}{10368}\nu^{4}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\delta\Biggl[\frac{405}{256}+\nu\left(-\frac{6889}{384}+\frac{41}{64}\pi^{2}\right)+\frac{93}{128}\nu^{2}+\frac{7}{1728}\nu^{3}\Biggr]\Biggr\}

\displaystyle+x^{5}\Biggl\{-\frac{1701}{512}+\nu\left(-\frac{329}{15}+\frac{1291}{1024}\pi^{2}+\frac{64}{5}\gamma_{\text{E}}+\frac{32}{5}\ln(16 x)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\nu^{2}\left(-\frac{1019179}{23040}+\frac{6703}{3072}\pi^{2}+\frac{64}{15}\gamma_{\text{E}}+\frac{32}{15}\ln(16 x)\right)+\nu^{3}\left(\frac{356551}{6912}-\frac{2255}{1152}\pi^{2}\right)-\frac{5621}{41472}\nu^{4}-\frac{187}{62208}\nu^{5}

\displaystyle\qquad+\delta\Biggl[\frac{1701}{512}+\nu\left(\frac{24689}{3840}-\frac{1291}{1024}\pi^{2}-\frac{64}{5}\gamma_{\text{E}}-\frac{32}{5}\ln(16 x)\right)+\nu^{2}\left(\frac{71207}{1536}-\frac{451}{256}\pi^{2}\right)-\frac{43}{576}\nu^{3}-\frac{55}{41472}\nu^{4}\Biggr]\Biggr\}\,.

I find that the latter result is in perfect agreement
9
at 4PN with Eq. (C3) of Ref.
29
and with Eq. (440) of Ref.
30
.

VIII
Application: gauge-invariant expressions for the 3PN fluxes

In Refs.
8
;
9
;
7
, the complete
10
10

10

In Ref.
9
, it was claimed that the memory contribution to the angular momentum flux was divergent when integrated up to the infinite past. It was then argued from astrophysical arguments that physical systems only form at a finite time in the past; this divergent contribution was then conventionally taken to vanish. Later, I have shown rigorously that this memory contribution is in fact always finite, even when integrating to the infinite past; see Appendix A of Ref.
104
. The contribution is non-zero in general, but vanishes after orbit-averaging.
orbit-averaged fluxes of energy
\langle\mathcal{F}\rangle
and angular momentum
\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle
were obtained for nonspinning, structureless, eccentric systems at 3PN order in terms of the quasi-Keplerian variables
(x,e_{t})
. These variables are gauge-dependent, and results were obtained both in the harmonic and ADM gauges. I have not rederived these results, and in the rest of this section, I will trust that these expressions are correct.
Instead, I will use my new mapping between energy and angular momentum to express these fluxes, for the
first time,
8

in terms of gauge-invariant frequencies
(x,\iota)
.

One first introduces the variable
e_{t}
, which here denotes the time-eccentricity in
ADM coordinates
11
11

11

Note that in modified harmonic coordinates, one finds a different expression for
e_{t}
, given by Eq. (25d) of Ref.
81
. Differences between the two coordinates arise only at 2PN, whereas hereditary terms need to be controlled at a relative 1.5PN order with respect to the leading tail. For this reason, hereditary fluxes are given in the literature without specifying the coordinate system.

entering the 3PN quasi-Keplerian parametrization
81
. It is given as an explicit function of
(\varepsilon,j)
in Eq. (20d) of Ref.
81
. The starting point will be the 3PN expressions of the orbit-averaged fluxes in ADM coordinates, which are provided in terms of
(x,e_{t})
. In the literature, these fluxes are further split into an instantaneous and a hereditary part. The energy flux is provided in Ref.
7
, see Eqs. (8.18)–(8.20) for the instantaneous part and Eqs. (8.11)–(8.13) for the hereditary part; see also
8
for the hereditary part. The angular momentum flux is provided in
9
, see Eqs. (4.10) and (4.11) for the instantaneous part and Eqs. (5.29) and (4.12) for the hereditary part.

A few remarks about these results are in order. First, these expressions of these fluxes feature the enhancement functions
F(e)
and
\tilde{F}(e)
, which have been replaced by their
exact
expressions, given by Eq. (8.13) of Ref.
7
and Eq. (5.24) of Ref.
9
, respectively. This allows for the arbitrary constant
x_{0}
, which appears separately in the instantaneous and hereditary fluxes, to cancel out explicitly in the total flux. Secondly, the hereditary pieces of these fluxes feature a collection of enhancement functions which are not known in closed form. These are defined exactly as infinite sums over the Fourier coefficients
12
12

12

The expressions for the Fourier coefficients of the current-type quadrupole moment
\mathrm{J}_{ij}
provided in the literature all have typos. In Ref.
79
, Eq. (A8) is lacking a total minus sign and should read
{}_{(p)}\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{zy}=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(1-e^{2})\frac{J_{p}(pe)}{pe}
. In the Appendix of Ref.
8
, there appears to be a problem in the factorization of the
-1/4
factor in Eq. (A5a), which would instead read

{}_{(p)}\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{xz}^{\text{(N)}}=-\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{1-e^{2}}\Biggl\{3eJ_{p}(pe)-(1+e^{2})\Bigl[J_{p+1}(pe)+J_{p-1}(pe)\Bigr]+\frac{e}{2}\Bigl[J_{p+2}(pe)+J_{p-2}(pe)\Bigr]\Biggr\}\ .

of multipolar moments, but are most practically approximated in closed form using small eccentricity expansions. Using asymptotic analysis, one can determine the
e\rightarrow 1
behavior or the enhancement functions, which makes it possible to engineer sensible approximations valid for both small and large eccentricities

79
; see Sec.

. For the reader’s convenience, in Table

Note that even when keeping only 3PN terms in the fluxes, one will find traces of the 4PN equations of motion when working in these variables, e.g., the presence of the enhancement function
\lambda_{0}(e)
. This is of course linked to the fact that at Newtonian order, the two frequencies
\omega
and
n
are degenerate — one needs the 1PN equations of motion to find a finite value for
\iota=\frac{3x}{\omega/n-1}
and control the pair of variables
(x,\iota)
at leading order. Thus, I find that the gauge-invariant expressions for the fluxes in terms of
